<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:main}
Let \(\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3\) be a bounded \(C^\infty\) domain,
whose boundary may have more than one component, and let
\(\lambda_j,\mu_j\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega;\mathbb R)\),
\(j=1,2\), satisfy \eqref{eq:convexity} on \(\overline\Omega\).
If their full Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps agree on the entire boundary,
\[
 \Lambda_{\lambda_1,\mu_1}^\Omega=\Lambda_{\lambda_2,\mu_2}^\Omega
 \quad\text{as maps }H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3)
 \longrightarrow H^{-1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3),
\]
then
\[
 \lambda_1=\lambda_2,\qquad \mu_1=\mu_2
 \quad\text{in }\Omega.
\]
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:main-scope}
Theorem~\ref{thm:main} establishes global uniqueness for
smooth, strongly convex isotropic elastic media in three
dimensions, without smallness, symmetry, separability, or
analyticity assumptions on the Lam\'e parameters.
The boundary of \(\Omega\) may have several connected
components, with the full displacement--traction response
available on each of them. In particular, the matrix
identities underlying the proof are derived entirely
from the physical Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and require
no additional measurements.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:main-method}

A central difficulty in establishing global uniqueness is
to extract sufficient matrix information from the physical
displacement--traction data to compare the two elastic
systems. Although the elasticity equations admit an exact
four-component Laplace-type reduction, equality of the
physical Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps does not imply equality
of the unrestricted boundary maps of the reduced systems.
Moreover, the leading physical adjoint lift provides only
two independent polarizations in a rank-three characteristic
quotient. Thus, the leading-order construction alone does
not yield the complete matrix identities required for
coefficient recovery.

The approach developed here overcomes this obstruction by
exploiting the first nonvanishing correction to a reduced
amplitude annihilated by the leading physical adjoint lift.
This produces a third independent physical adjoint
polarization and completes an invertible quotient frame
(Theorem~\ref{thm:graded-frame}).

A distinguishing feature of the argument is that the
resulting complete matrix identities are derived directly
from the physical elastic equations. Using exact physical
CGO solutions and the Betti identity, with primal and
adjoint quotient frames fixed independently of the test
weight, one obtains a full \(3\times3\) matrix integral
identity for every holomorphic polynomial weight
(Theorem~\ref{thm:physical-nine-moments}).

These identities provide the contour compatibility needed
for a Cauchy--Plemelj construction of an invertible
relative transport normalized in the common exterior,
without requiring the individual transport frames to
satisfy an exterior normalization
(Proposition~\ref{prop:complete-moment-relative-transport}).
Its global construction and projective holomorphicity
(Theorem~\ref{thm:quotient-reconstruction}), combined with
the rigidity of the Lam\'e quotient connection
(Theorem~\ref{thm:rigidity}), complete the simultaneous
determination of both Lam\'e parameters.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:frozen-conventions}
The boundary duality pairings between $H^{-1/2}$ and
$H^{1/2}$, as well as the volume pairings used below,
are taken complex-bilinearly, without complex
conjugation. For matrices, the superscript $T$ denotes
the ordinary transpose without complex conjugation.
For differential operators, it denotes the formal
transpose with respect to the complex-bilinear volume
pairing; in particular, $(\partial_k)^T=-\partial_k$. 

For \(\theta=e_1+\mathrm i e_2\), with real orthonormal
vectors \(e_1,e_2\), set \(\nu_\theta=e_1\times e_2\) and
\(x_\perp=\nu_\theta\cdot x\). We use the leaf coordinate
\(z=e_1\cdot x+\mathrm i e_2\cdot x\). Then
\(D_\theta=\theta\cdot\nabla=2\partial_{\bar z}\).

The dual quotient connection \eqref{eq:Gamma} has
lower-left block \(-\theta^TF_\mu Q\).
Projective dependence is encoded by the bundle transition
matrix \eqref{eq:spinor-transition} and the transport
covariance laws
\eqref{eq:G-chart-covariance}--\eqref{eq:H-chart-covariance}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:factorization}
On smooth four-component fields, the Lam\'e operator admits
the following exact identity of differential operators:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:factorization}
 L_{\mu,\kappa}T_\mu=S_{\mu,\kappa}M_{\mu,\kappa},
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:M-explicit}
 M_{\mu,\kappa}
 \binom vf
 =
 \begin{pmatrix}
 \Delta v-m\,\operatorname{div} v+F_\mu\nabla f+V_\mu v+F_\mu m\,f
 \\
 \Delta f+d_{\mu,\kappa}\operatorname{div} v-e_{\mu,\kappa}m\cdot v+q_\mu f
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:M-Laplace-type}
 M_{\mu,\kappa}=\DeltaI_4+\sum_{k=1}^3A_k\partial_k+B,
\end{equation}
with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ak}
 A_k=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 -m e_k^T&F_\mu e_k\\
 \kappa\sqrt\mu\,e_k^T&0
 \end{pmatrix}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Bmatrix}
 B=
 \begin{pmatrix}
 V_\mu&F_\mu m\\[1mm]
 -\dfrac{3\kappa-2}{2}\sqrt\mu\,m^T&q_\mu
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Here \(e_k\) is the \(k\)-th standard coordinate vector in
\(\mathbb R^3\). Both \(A_k\) and \(B\) are \(4\times4\) matrix
fields in the \(3+1\) block decomposition, and the Laplacian
acts componentwise.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Set
\[
 a=\mu^{-1}=c^2,\qquad \mathsf K=\lambda+\mu,\qquad \Sigma=\lambda+2\mu.
\]
The transformation \(T_\mu(v,f)\) decomposes into the
vector and scalar-potential contributions
\[
T_\mu(v,f)=u^{(v)}+u^{(f)},
\qquad
u^{(v)}=cv,
\qquad
u^{(f)}=a\nabla f-f\nabla a.
\]
Here the superscripts \((v)\) and \((f)\) label the
contributions associated with the vector field \(v\)
and the scalar function \(f\), respectively.

For the scalar-potential contribution, set
\[
p_f=\Delta f-\frac{\Delta a}{a}f.
\]
The mixed first-derivative terms cancel in the
symmetric gradient, yielding
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar-div-eps}
\operatorname{div} u^{(f)}
 =a\Delta f-f\Delta a
 =a p_f,
\qquad
\varepsilon(u^{(f)})
 =a\nabla^2 f-f\nabla^2 a.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:scalar-div-eps} and \(a\mu=1\), the corresponding stress tensor is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-scalar-stress}
 \sigma(u^{(f)})
 =a\lambda p_fI+2\nabla^2f-2\mu f\nabla^2a.
\end{equation}
Take the divergence of \eqref{eq:proof-scalar-stress}, using
\(
 \sum_{j=1}^3\partial_j(\partial_i\partial_j a)
 =\partial_i\Delta a
\)
and
\(
 \nabla\mu=-\mu^2\nabla a
\), and obtain the exact identity
\begin{align}
 L_{\lambda,\mu}u^{(f)}
 ={}&\nabla\bigl(a\Sigma p_f\bigr)
 +2\mu\bigl(\Delta a\,I-\nabla^2a\bigr)\nabla f \label{eq:L-scalar-part}\\
 &\quad
 +2\mu^2\bigl(\nabla^2a-\Delta a\,I\bigr)\nabla a\,f.\notag
\end{align}
Now
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-reciprocal-derivatives}
 \nabla a=-am,
 \qquad
 \nabla^2a=a(m\otimes m-\nabla^2\ell),
 \qquad
 \frac{\Delta a}{a}=|m|^2-\Delta\ell.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:proof-reciprocal-derivatives}, with
\[
 \mathcal H_\mu
 =\nabla^2\ell-m\otimes m+\bigl(|m|^2-\Delta\ell\bigr)I,
\]
we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-scalar-hessian-conversion}
 \Delta a\,I-\nabla^2a=a\mathcal H_\mu,
 \qquad
 F_\mu=2c\mathcal H_\mu.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:proof-scalar-hessian-conversion} and \eqref{eq:proof-reciprocal-derivatives}, the two non-gradient terms in \eqref{eq:L-scalar-part} equal
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-scalar-nongradient-part}
 c^{-1}F_\mu\nabla f+c^{-1}F_\mu m\,f.
\end{equation}
Moreover, by \eqref{eq:sum-relations}, \eqref{eq:qmu}, and \eqref{eq:proof-reciprocal-derivatives},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-scalar-gradient-coefficients}
 a\Sigma=\frac1{1-\kappa},
 \qquad
 -\frac{\Delta a}{a}=q_\mu,
\end{equation}
so \eqref{eq:proof-scalar-gradient-coefficients} converts the gradient term in \eqref{eq:L-scalar-part} to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:scalar-gradient-part}
 \nabla\left(\frac{\Delta f+q_\mu f}{1-\kappa}\right).
\end{equation}

For the vector contribution \(u^{(v)}=cv\), we first expand
the elasticity operator. By direct differentiation of the stress
for a smooth vector field \(u\), we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:L-general-expanded}
 L_{\lambda,\mu}u
 =\mu\Delta u+\mathsf K\nabla\operatorname{div} u+(\operatorname{div} u)\nabla\lambda
 +\bigl(\nabla u+(\nabla u)^T\bigr)\nabla\mu.
\end{equation}
Substitute \(u=cv\) into \eqref{eq:L-general-expanded}, use
\(
 \mu=c^{-2}
\)
and
\(
 \nabla\mu=-2c^{-3}\nabla c
\), and subtract the gradient
\[
 \nabla\left(c\mathsf K\operatorname{div} v+(\lambda-\mu)\nabla c\cdot v\right),
\]
after which the mixed first-derivative terms cancel by
\(2\mu\nabla c+c\nabla\mu=0\) and
\(\mathsf K\nabla c+c\nabla\mu=(\lambda-\mu)\nabla c\).
The coefficient of \(\operatorname{div} v\) that remains is
\(c(\nabla\lambda-\nabla \mathsf K)=-c\nabla\mu=-c^{-1}m\).
Thus
\begin{align}
 L_{\lambda,\mu}(cv)
 ={}&\nabla\left(c\mathsf K\operatorname{div} v+(\lambda-\mu)\nabla c\cdot v\right)\label{eq:L-vector-part}\\*
 &\quad+c^{-1}\bigl(\Delta v-m\operatorname{div} v+V_\mu v\bigr).\notag
\end{align}
The zeroth-order coefficient in \eqref{eq:L-vector-part}
is obtained from the identity
\[
 -\bigl(2\nabla^2(c^{-1})+\Delta(c^{-1})I\bigr)
 =c^{-1}V_\mu,
\]
which follows from \eqref{eq:Vmu} and
\eqref{eq:proof-inverse-c-derivatives}:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-inverse-c-derivatives}
 \nabla^2(c^{-1})
 =c^{-1}\left(\frac12\nabla^2\ell+\frac14m\otimes m\right),
 \qquad
 \Delta(c^{-1})
 =c^{-1}\left(\frac12\Delta\ell+\frac14|m|^2\right).
\end{equation}
Finally, using \eqref{eq:sum-relations} and
\(
 \nabla c=-\frac12cm
\), we obtain
\begin{align}
 c\mathsf K\operatorname{div} v
 &=\frac{\kappa\sqrt\mu}{1-\kappa}\operatorname{div} v,\label{eq:vector-gradient-convert1}\\
 (\lambda-\mu)\nabla c\cdot v
 &=-\frac{3\kappa-2}{2(1-\kappa)}\sqrt\mu\,m\cdot v.\label{eq:vector-gradient-convert2}
\end{align}
By \eqref{eq:vector-gradient-convert1}--\eqref{eq:vector-gradient-convert2} and \eqref{eq:dmu}--\eqref{eq:emu}, the gradient in \eqref{eq:L-vector-part} is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:vector-gradient-part}
 \nabla\left(
 \frac{d_{\mu,\kappa}\operatorname{div} v-e_{\mu,\kappa}m\cdot v}{1-\kappa}
 \right).
\end{equation}

Combine \eqref{eq:proof-scalar-nongradient-part} and
\eqref{eq:scalar-gradient-part} with \eqref{eq:L-vector-part} and
\eqref{eq:vector-gradient-part}, using \eqref{eq:M-explicit}, to obtain
\[
 L_{\mu,\kappa}T_\mu(v,f)
 =\sqrt\mu\,(M_{\mu,\kappa}(v,f))_{1:3}
 +\nabla\left(\frac{(M_{\mu,\kappa}(v,f))_4}{1-\kappa}\right),
\]
which is precisely \eqref{eq:factorization} by \eqref{eq:Smu}.  We collect the first- and zeroth-order terms in \eqref{eq:M-explicit} to obtain \eqref{eq:Ak} and \eqref{eq:Bmatrix}.  \qedhere
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
The factorization maps every smooth solution of
\(M_{\mu,\kappa}(v,f)=0\) to a physical elastic solution
\(u=T_\mu(v,f)\).
The rigidity argument below uses only the first-order
matrices \(A_k\). The zeroth-order matrix \(B\) is essential
for constructing exact CGO solutions.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:factorization-sign-audit}
With \(a=\mu^{-1}=c^2\), the following coefficient identities
provide a direct sign check:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:factorization-sign-checks}
\begin{aligned}
 \nabla(\mu^{-1})&=-\mu^{-1}m,&
 \Delta(\mu^{-1})/\mu^{-1}&=|m|^2-\Delta\ell,&
 \nabla c&=-\tfrac12cm,\\
 a(\lambda+2\mu)&=(1-\kappa)^{-1},&
 c(\lambda+\mu)&=\frac{\kappa\sqrt\mu}{1-\kappa},\\
 (\lambda-\mu)\nabla c
 &=-\frac{3\kappa-2}{2(1-\kappa)}\sqrt\mu\,m.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

The six coefficient identities in \eqref{eq:factorization-sign-checks} fix the signs of $q_\mu$, the lower-left entry of $B$, and
the first-order lower-left block of $A_k$.  In the constant-coefficient case
\(m=0\), one has $F_\mu=V_\mu=q_\mu=0$ and
\[
 M_{\mu,\kappa}(v,f)=
 \binom{\Delta v}{\Delta f+\kappa\sqrt\mu\,\operatorname{Div}v},
\]
which is the stated constant-coefficient specialization.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:bg-covariance}
Let \(\theta\in\mathbb C^3\setminus\{0\}\) satisfy
\(\theta\cdot\theta=0\), and let
\[
 \mathcal T_\theta=2D_\theta+A_\theta,
 \qquad
 \widetilde{\mathcal T}_\theta=2D_\theta+\widetilde A_\theta
\]
be the leading transport operators associated with \(M\) and \(\widetilde M=\mathcal R^{-1}M\mathcal R\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport-covariance}
 \widetilde{\mathcal T}_\theta
 =\mathcal R^{-1}\mathcal T_\theta\mathcal R.
\end{equation}
The characteristic line and the primal quotient are
transported by \(\mathcal R^{-1}\), and the dual quotient
by \(\mathcal R^T\). In particular, the original generator
\(e_\theta=(\theta,0)^T\) is replaced by
\[
 \widetilde e_\theta=\mathcal R^{-1}e_\theta
 =\binom{\theta}{\dfrac{d_*}{2}x\cdot\theta},
\]
whose line is preserved by \(\widetilde{\mathcal T}_\theta\).
If \(X\) and \(Y\) are ambient representatives of primal
and dual quotient frames for the original reduction, their
conjugated representatives may be chosen as
\[
 \widetilde X=\mathcal R^{-1}X,
 \qquad
 \widetilde Y=\mathcal R^TY.
\]
In these transformed frames, with their spatial derivatives
included, the coordinate quotient connection matrices are
unchanged. For two media conjugated by the same \(\mathcal R\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:moment-covariance}
 \widetilde Y_2^T
 (\widetilde A_{1,\theta}-\widetilde A_{2,\theta})
 \widetilde X_1
 =
 Y_2^T(A_{1,\theta}-A_{2,\theta})X_1.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
The coefficient of the first-order derivative in \(\mathcal R^{-1}M\mathcal R\) is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-conjugated-first-order-coefficients}
 \widetilde A_k
 =\mathcal R^{-1}A_k\mathcal R
 +2\mathcal R^{-1}(\partial_k\mathcal R).
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:proof-conjugated-first-order-coefficients},
for every smooth four-component amplitude \(w\),
\[
 (2D_\theta+\widetilde A_\theta)w
 =\mathcal R^{-1}(2D_\theta+A_\theta)(\mathcal Rw),
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:transport-covariance}. The companion
identity for a dual amplitude \(y\) is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-dual-background-covariance}
 (2D_\theta-\widetilde A_\theta^T)(\mathcal R^Ty)
 =\mathcal R^T(2D_\theta-A_\theta^T)y.
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:transport-covariance} and
\eqref{eq:proof-dual-background-covariance} transport the original characteristic line
and the paired quotient equations to the conjugated system.
The paired transformations in Proposition~\ref{prop:bg-covariance} also preserve the bilinear pairing, since
\((\mathcal R^Ty)^T(\mathcal R^{-1}w)=y^Tw\).

To verify the coordinate connection statement explicitly,
we choose \(\mathsf U_\theta\) as a dual quotient basis and
\(\mathsf V_\theta\) as a paired primal lift, both independent
of \(x\), with \(\mathsf U_\theta^T\mathsf V_\theta=I_3\).
Set \(\widetilde{\mathsf U}_\theta=\mathcal R^T\mathsf U_\theta\)
and \(\widetilde{\mathsf V}_\theta=\mathcal R^{-1}\mathsf V_\theta\).
The induced connection in the paired frames
\(\widetilde{\mathsf U}_\theta\) and
\(\widetilde{\mathsf V}_\theta\), which now depend on \(x\), is
\[
 \widetilde{\mathsf U}_\theta^T
 \bigl(2D_\theta\widetilde{\mathsf V}_\theta
       +\widetilde A_\theta\widetilde{\mathsf V}_\theta\bigr)
 =\mathsf U_\theta^TA_\theta\mathsf V_\theta.
\]
The frame derivative on the left is essential;
\eqref{eq:transport-covariance} gives the equality including this term.

Finally, we use the same \(\mathcal R\) for both media to cancel
the derivative terms and obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-background-coefficient-difference}
 \widetilde A_{1,\theta}-\widetilde A_{2,\theta}
 =\mathcal R^{-1}(A_{1,\theta}-A_{2,\theta})\mathcal R.
\end{equation}
Use \eqref{eq:proof-background-coefficient-difference} with
\(\widetilde X_1=\mathcal R^{-1}X_1\) and
\(\widetilde Y_2=\mathcal R^TY_2\) to prove
\eqref{eq:moment-covariance}.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:holomorphic-lifts}
On every sufficiently small spinor-coordinate neighborhood,
the right inverse \(\mathsf V_\theta\) may be chosen
holomorphically in that coordinate and independently of
\(x\) and the medium. On a chart overlap, any such local
choices satisfy
\[
 \mathsf V_0=\mathsf V_\infty D^{-T}
             +e_{\theta_0}\mathsf L
\]
locally for a holomorphic \(1\times3\) row \(\mathsf L\).
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
The entries of \(\mathsf U_\theta^T\) are holomorphic
and the matrix has full row rank three. Locally, we choose a
nonvanishing \(3\times3\) minor, invert it, and set the
remaining ambient coordinate to zero. We thereby construct a
holomorphic right inverse with no dependence on \(x\) or
the coefficients.
On an overlap, \eqref{eq:proof-dual-frame-transition} gives
\[
 \mathsf U_0^T(\mathsf V_\infty D^{-T})
 =D^T\mathsf U_\infty^T\mathsf V_\infty D^{-T}=I_3.
\]
Thus the difference of the two right inverses has image in
\(\ker\mathsf U_0^T=\mathbb C e_{\theta_0}\).
Since \(e_{\theta_0}\) is a nonvanishing holomorphic
generator, that difference is locally \(e_{\theta_0}\mathsf L\)
with \(\mathsf L\) holomorphic.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:transport-lift}
Let \(X\) be a smooth \(3\times r\) matrix amplitude
satisfying
\[
 (2D_\theta+\mathcal A_\theta)X=0.
\]
On every sufficiently small leafwise product neighborhood,
there is a smooth \(1\times r\) row \(\alpha\) such that
\[
 Z=\mathsf V_\theta X+e_\theta\alpha,
 \qquad
 (2D_\theta+A_\theta)Z=0,
 \qquad
 \mathsf U_\theta^TZ=X.
\]
For a single column, \(r=1\) and \(\alpha\) is a scalar;
a quotient frame corresponds to \(r=3\).
If \(X\), the quotient basis, and the direction depend
smoothly on auxiliary parameters, \(\alpha\) and \(Z\)
may be chosen locally smoothly in those parameters.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Equation~\eqref{eq:proof-quotient-transport-projection} gives
\[
 \mathsf U_\theta^T(2D_\theta+A_\theta)\mathsf V_\theta X
 =(2D_\theta+\mathcal A_\theta)X=0.
\]
The residual therefore lies columnwise in
\(\ker\mathsf U_\theta^T=\mathbb C e_\theta\). On a small direction
neighborhood, we choose a smooth row \(\ell_\theta\) with
\(\ell_\theta e_\theta=1\). Then the smooth \(1\times r\) row
\(f=\ell_\theta(2D_\theta+A_\theta)\mathsf V_\theta X\)
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-characteristic-transport-residual}
 (2D_\theta+A_\theta)\mathsf V_\theta X=e_\theta f.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:proof-characteristic-transport-residual} and
\((2D_\theta+A_\theta)e_\theta=0\), it remains to
solve \(2D_\theta\alpha=-f\).

For the normalization, we choose a locally smooth decomposition
\(\theta=b\theta_{\mathrm n}\), where \(b\ne0\) is independent
of \(x\), and \(\theta_{\mathrm n}=e_1+\mathrm i e_2\)
with real orthonormal \(e_1,e_2\).
In the leaf coordinate \(z=e_1\cdot x+\mathrm i e_2\cdot x\),
\(D_\theta=2b\partial_{\bar z}\).
Pull back to fixed disks \(D\Subset D_1\) contained in the
neighborhood where \(X\) is defined, and choose
\(\chi\in C_c^\infty(D_1)\) equal to one near
\(\overline D\). With \(x_\perp\) the transverse coordinate, we set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-characteristic-cauchy-correction}
 \alpha(z,x_\perp)
 =-\frac{1}{4\pi b}\int_{D_1}
       \frac{\chi(w)f(w,x_\perp)}{z-w}\,dA(w).
\end{equation}
The identity \(\partial_{\bar z}(\pi^{-1}z^{-1})=\delta_0\)
applied to \eqref{eq:proof-characteristic-cauchy-correction}
gives \(2D_\theta\alpha=-f\) on \(D\).
The Cauchy solution is spatially smooth for the smooth
cutoff source. Locally in the auxiliary parameters, the
disks and cutoff may be fixed, while \(b^{-1}\),
\(\ell_\theta\), and the pulled-back source vary smoothly.
Parameter derivatives commute with the fixed integral,
which gives the asserted local smooth dependence.
Finally, \(\mathsf U_\theta^T\mathsf V_\theta=I_3\) and
\(\mathsf U_\theta^Te_\theta=0\) give
\(\mathsf U_\theta^TZ=X\).
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:ER-solvability}
Let \(m\) be a positive integer and let
\[
 \widetilde M=\DeltaI_m
              +\sum_{j=1}^3\widetilde A_j(x)\partial_j
              +\widetilde B(x),
 \qquad \widetilde A_j,\widetilde B
        \in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^3;M_m(\mathbb C)).
\]
Write \(\widetilde A=(\widetilde A_1,\widetilde A_2,\widetilde A_3)\)
and \(\widetilde A_\theta=\sum_j\theta_j\widetilde A_j\),
and fix concentric balls \(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\Subset\Omega_1\Subset\Omega_0\).
Here and below, a normalized complex null direction means
\(\theta=e_1+\mathrm i e_2\), where \(e_1,e_2\in\mathbb R^3\) are
orthonormal.  In particular \(\theta\ne0\) and \(\theta\cdot\theta=0\).
Choose a closed concentric ball \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\Subset\Omega_1\) whose interior
contains \(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\) and the coefficient support, and let
\(\Theta\) be a compact family of such directions.  For
any fixed integer \(k\geq0\), there are \(h_0=h_0(k)>0\) and linear
operators \(R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}\), defined for \(0<h<h_0\), such that, for
\(f\in H^k(\Omega_0;\mathbb C^m)\) with \(\operatorname{supp} f\subset K_{\mathrm{src}}\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse-equation}
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}\widetilde M e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}
 R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}f=f\quad\hbox{in }\Omega_1
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse}
 h^{-1}\|R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}f\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}+
 \|R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}f\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega_0)}
 \leq C_k\|f\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}.
\end{equation}
The constants are uniform in \(\theta\in\Theta\), in the sense specified
in Remark~\ref{rem:CGO-uniformity}.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
The argument uses the localized right inverse constructed in
Proposition~\ref{prop:appendix-interior-right-inverse} of
Appendix~\ref{audrev-ordinary-parametrix}. Its construction
relies on the free weighted inverse of
Lemma~\ref{lem:appendix-free-inverse} and the localized
parametrix of Lemma~\ref{lem:appendix-local-error}.


To check conventions, we multiply \(\widetilde M\) by \(-1\)
and set
\[
 A^{\mathrm{ER}}_j=\frac{\widetilde A_j}{2\mathrm i},\qquad
 B^{\mathrm{ER}}=-\widetilde B,\qquad
 \theta_{\mathrm{ER}}=\mathrm i\theta,\qquad \tau=h^{-1}.
\]
The operator in \cite[equation (1.1)]{EskinRalstonSystems2004} is \(-\widetilde M\).
We take the auxiliary Fourier vector in \cite{EskinRalstonSystems2004} to be \(l=0\), which gives
\(\delta=\tau\theta_{\mathrm{ER}}\), so the phase
\(e^{\mathrm i\delta\cdot x}\) is \(e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}\).
Equation (1.6) of \cite{EskinRalstonSystems2004} becomes
\[
 (2D_\theta+\widetilde A_\theta)C=0.
\]
The conjugated operator in this convention is the negative of
\(e^{\theta\cdot x/h}\widetilde M e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}\),
so its source is \(-f\). The inverse constructed in
Appendix~\ref{audrev-ordinary-parametrix} gives
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse-equation}.



For sources supported in \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\), the
localized right-inverse identity holds in \(\Omega_1\),
while the estimates are taken over \(\Omega_0\).
Both Sobolev bounds in \eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse},
at orders \(k\) and \(k+1\), are obtained from the same
Neumann-series inverse acting on \(H^k\).
Uniformity over compact families of normalized null
directions follows from the finite covering by local
transport frames. The rotation argument below provides
an alternative verification of this uniformity.



Translate the domains and coefficients together so that
the common center of the balls is the origin.
Fix a reference normalized null direction
\(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}\). Locally in \(\theta\), we choose a
smooth rotation \(O_\theta\in SO(3)\) with
\(O_\theta\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}=\theta\), and define the
pullback \((\mathcal P_\theta f)(y)=f(O_\theta y)\).
The balls and \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\) are preserved. The direction becomes
\(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}\), and the coefficients become
\[
 \mathsf A_{\theta,j}(y)
 =\sum_l\widetilde A_l(O_\theta y)(O_\theta)_{lj},
 \qquad \mathsf B_\theta(y)=\widetilde B(O_\theta y).
\]
The pulled-back conjugated operator is
\[
 L_{h,\theta}=\Delta-2h^{-1}D_{\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}}
   +\mathsf A_\theta\cdot\nabla
   -h^{-1}\mathsf A_{\theta,\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}}
   +\mathsf B_\theta,
 \qquad
 \mathsf A_{\theta,\theta_{\mathrm{ref}}}
 =\sum_j(\theta_{\mathrm{ref}})_j\mathsf A_{\theta,j}.
\]
On the closed source space
\(\mathcal E_k=\{f\in H^k(\Omega_0;\mathbb C^m):\operatorname{supp} f\subset K_{\mathrm{src}}\}\),
choose a localized right inverse
\(\mathsf R_{h,\theta^0}\) for the operator at a base
parameter \(\theta^0\).
Its target norm is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-right-inverse-target-norm}
 \|u\|_{\mathcal X_h^k}
 =h^{-1}\|u\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}+\|u\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega_0)}.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse} and
\eqref{eq:proof-right-inverse-target-norm}, the norm of
\(\mathsf R_{h,\theta^0}\) is bounded independently of \(h\). Since the principal direction is fixed after
rotation, \(D_{h,\theta}=L_{h,\theta}-L_{h,\theta^0}\)
has coefficients supported in \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-operator-perturbation}
 \|D_{h,\theta}u\|_{H^k}
 \leq C\bigl(\|\mathsf A_\theta-\mathsf A_{\theta^0}\|_{C^{k+1}}
           +\|\mathsf B_\theta-\mathsf B_{\theta^0}\|_{C^{k+1}}\bigr)
          \|u\|_{\mathcal X_h^k},\qquad 0<h\leq1.
\end{equation}
In particular, the factor \(h^{-1}\) in \(D_{h,\theta}\) is controlled by
\(h^{-1}\|u\|_{H^k}\) in \eqref{eq:proof-right-inverse-target-norm}.
By \eqref{eq:proof-cgo-operator-perturbation} and
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse}, \(Q_{h,\theta}=D_{h,\theta}\mathsf R_{h,\theta^0}\)
maps \(\mathcal E_k\) to itself and has norm at most
\(1/2\) on a direction neighborhood independent of \(h\).
Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-perturbed-right-inverse}
 \mathsf R_{h,\theta}
 =\mathsf R_{h,\theta^0}(I+Q_{h,\theta})^{-1}.
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse-equation}, applied at the
base parameter in the rotated coordinates, holds throughout \(\Omega_1\),
which contains the support of \(D_{h,\theta}\). Using
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse-equation} and
\eqref{eq:proof-cgo-perturbed-right-inverse}, we obtain
\[
 L_{h,\theta}\mathsf R_{h,\theta}f
 =(I+Q_{h,\theta})(I+Q_{h,\theta})^{-1}f=f
 \quad\text{in }\Omega_1.
\]
Its norm into \(\mathcal X_h^k\) is at most twice the base
norm. We return to the original coordinates by setting
\[
 R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}
 =\mathcal P_\theta^{-1}\mathsf R_{h,\theta}\mathcal P_\theta.
\]
Rotations give uniformly equivalent Sobolev norms on the
fixed balls. We use a finite cover of \(\Theta\) to obtain a common
\(h_0(k)\leq1\) and \(C_k\). We combine these local operators with
a parameter partition of unity after they have been returned
to the original coordinates. Its weights are independent
of \(x\), so the right-inverse equation is preserved.
No parameter derivative of this family is asserted or needed.
The construction uses the exterior extension to \(\mathbb R^3\),
rather than a bounded-domain Dirichlet inverse.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:cauchy-leaf-estimate}
Let \(D\Subset D_1\Subset\mathbb C\) be disks, \(I\Subset\mathbb R\) an interval,
and \(0<\alpha<1\).  In coordinates in which
\(D_\theta=2\partial_{\bar z}\), set
\[
 (\mathcal C_\theta F)(z,x_\perp)=\frac1{2\pi}\int_{D_1}
                   \frac{F(w,x_\perp)}{z-w}\,dA(w).
\]
If \(F\) is smooth and supported away from \(\partial D_1\times I\), then
\[
 D_\theta\mathcal C_\theta F=F\quad\hbox{on }D\times I.
\]
Moreover, for all nonnegative integers \(r,q\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:anisotropic-cauchy}
 \|\mathcal C_\theta F\|_{C_{x_\perp}^qC_z^{r+1,\alpha}(D\times I)}
 \leq C_{r,q}\|F\|_{C_{x_\perp}^qC_z^{r,\alpha}(D_1\times I)}.
\end{equation}
Here \(C_{x_\perp}^qC_z^{r,\alpha}\) denotes \(q\) derivatives in the transverse
variable with values in the indicated leafwise Hölder space.  The estimate is
uniform on a compact directional chart after pulling the leaf coordinates
back by a smooth family of rotations.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The distributional identity
\(\partial_{\bar z}(\pi^{-1}z^{-1})=\delta_0\) proves the first assertion
with the factor \(1/(2\pi)\) in the definition of \(\mathcal C_\theta\).  For every fixed \(x_\perp\), the local
Schauder estimate for the Cauchy solution operator gives one derivative in
\(z\).  Derivatives in \(x_\perp\) commute with the integral, which proves
\eqref{eq:anisotropic-cauchy}.  The rotations and their derivatives are
bounded on a compact directional chart.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:autonomous-cgo}
Let \(\widetilde M\), the fixed domains, \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\), and \(\Theta\) be as in
Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability}.  Fix a nonnegative integer \(k\), and put
\[
 P_0=\widetilde M,\qquad T_\theta=2D_\theta+\widetilde A_\theta.
\]
Suppose that \(a_0\) is smooth near \(\overline\Omega_1\) and satisfies
\(T_\theta a_0=0\) there.  There exist a smooth first correction \(a_1\)
and an exact solution
\begin{equation}\label{eq:localized-cgo-package}
 \boldsymbol z_h=e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}(a_0+h a_1+r_h),\qquad
 \widetilde M\boldsymbol z_h=0\quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},
\end{equation}
such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:localized-cgo-remainder}
 T_\theta a_1=P_0a_0\quad\text{near }\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},\qquad
 \|r_h\|_{H^k(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\leq C_kh^2.
\end{equation}
Any prescribed smooth \(a_1\), defined near \(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\) and
satisfying this transport equation there, may instead be used.

The remainder bound \eqref{eq:localized-cgo-remainder} holds uniformly on a compact direction chart if the cutoffs are
fixed, the transport frames and their inverses are uniformly bounded, and
\(\|\chi_0P_0a_1\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}\) is uniformly bounded.
For the constructed correction, a sufficient condition is bounded
\(C_{x_\perp}^{k+4}C_z^{k+3,\alpha}\) norms of the coefficients, \(a_0\), and the
chosen frames, their inverses, and the fixed partition weights
on the fixed cylinder, for \(0<\alpha<1\).
For a prescribed correction its corresponding \(H^{k+2}\) bound on the
fixed cutoff support must also be assumed.  The application below takes
\(k=5\); thus \(C_{x_\perp}^9C_z^{8,\alpha}\) bounds suffice for the constructed
correction. For any constant vector \(v\in\mathbb C^m\), smooth
amplitudes \(a_0=C_\theta v\,b(x_\perp)g(z)\) are admissible,
where \(C_\theta\) is a transport frame on a fixed large
cylinder \(D_L\times I\), with \(L\) larger than the radius of
\(\Omega_1\), and \(b\in C_c^\infty(I)\). The product is extended
smoothly by zero outside \(I\) in the transverse variable.
Let \(U\) be a fixed neighborhood of \(\overline\Omega_1\)
whose slices with $x_\perp\in\operatorname{supp} b$ lie in the fixed transport cylinder $D_L\times I$.
The function \(g\) must be holomorphic on a neighborhood of
the entire planar projection of \(U\cap\{x_\perp\in\operatorname{supp} b\}\).
This gives a smooth leading amplitude on \(U\) satisfying its
transport equation, without requiring a single frame at transverse
coordinates where \(b=0\). Holomorphy only over the smaller working ball does not ensure holomorphy on the entire planar projection of $U\cap\{x_\perp\in\operatorname{supp} b\}$.
Every holomorphic polynomial meets the required domain condition.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The exact conjugation identity is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:conjugated-cgo-operator}
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}\widetilde M e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}
 =P_0-h^{-1}T_\theta.
\end{equation}

We choose a fixed cylinder containing \(\overline\Omega_1\), enlarged
for the planar cutoff. The leafwise transport inverse
\(\mathcal V_\theta\) constructed in
Appendix~\ref{audrev-ordinary-parametrix} uses frames on this entire
large leaf disk and a finite cover of the required transverse
interval.
Choose \(\chi\in C_c^\infty(\Omega_1)\), equal to one
near \(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\), and extend
\(\chi P_0a_0\) by zero to the cylinder. Set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-first-correction}
 a_1=\mathcal V_\theta(\chi P_0a_0).
\end{equation}
The transport right-inverse identity
\eqref{audrev-transport-inverse-identity}, where $\chi_{\rm leaf}=1$
on the target region, and Lemma~\ref{lem:cauchy-leaf-estimate},
applied to the finitely many summands of $\mathcal V_\theta$, give
\(T_\theta a_1=P_0a_0\) where \(\chi=1\).
The fixed transverse weights and their required derivatives are
included in the constants beside the finitely many frame and
inverse seminorms.

With the stated finite seminorm bounds, \(P_0a_0\) and
the cutoff source are bounded in
\(C_{x_\perp}^{k+2}C_z^{k+1,\alpha}\). The Cauchy inverse gains
one leaf derivative, so \(a_1\) is bounded in
\(C_{x_\perp}^{k+2}C_z^{k+2,\alpha}\).
A second application of \(P_0\) gives
\(\chi_0P_0a_1\in C_{x_\perp}^kC_z^{k,\alpha}\), hence a bounded
\(H^k\) norm on the fixed cutoff support used below.

Choose \(\chi_0\in C_c^\infty(\operatorname{int}K_{\mathrm{src}})\)
equal to one near \(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\), with support where
\(T_\theta a_1=P_0a_0\) holds. For a prescribed \(a_1\),
choose \(\operatorname{supp}\chi_0\) inside a neighborhood where that
correction and its transport equation are defined.
The product \(\chi_0P_0a_1\) extends smoothly by zero
to \(\Omega_0\) and is supported in \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\).  Since
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-uncorrected-residual}
 (P_0-h^{-1}T_\theta)(a_0+ha_1)=hP_0a_1
 \quad\text{near }\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},
\end{equation}
the correction
\[
 r_h=-R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}(h\chi_0P_0a_1)
\]
cancels \eqref{eq:proof-cgo-uncorrected-residual} by
\eqref{eq:conjugated-cgo-operator} and Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability},
makes \eqref{eq:localized-cgo-package} exact in \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\), and obeys
\[
 \|r_h\|_{H^k(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
 \leq C_kh\,\|h\chi_0P_0a_1\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}
 \leq C_kh^2.
\]
For each fixed \(h\), local elliptic regularity makes the exact field
smooth in \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\), because the coefficients and source are smooth.
The uniform bound for
\(\|\chi_0P_0a_1\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}\), together with
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse}, gives the asserted uniform
\(H^k\) remainder estimate. Multiplication of a leading transport
amplitude by \(b(x_\perp)g(z)\) preserves \(T_\theta a_0=0\), since
\(D_\theta b=D_\theta g=0\).
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:fast-physical-lift}
Let \(\boldsymbol z_h\) be an exact solution of the \emph{original} Lam\'e reduced
system \(M\boldsymbol z_h=0\), with expansion
\(\boldsymbol z_h=e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}(a_0+ha_1+r_h)\), where
\(a_0,a_1\) are smooth near \(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\) and
uniformly bounded in \(H^5(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})\), and
\(\|r_h\|_{H^5(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}=O(h^2)\).
Let \(K=S^TT\) be the original operator in Section~\ref{sec:reduction}.
For the background-conjugated construction this means
\(\boldsymbol z_h=\mathcal R\widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_h\), \(a_i=\mathcal R\widetilde a_i\), and
\(r_h=\mathcal R\widetilde r_h\), before applying \(K\).
The matrices \(\mathcal R^{\pm1}\) preserve the stated Sobolev orders on
the fixed balls.  Then the normalized physical adjoint field
\[
 y_h^{F}=-hK\boldsymbol z_h
\]
satisfies
\[
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}y_h^F=J_\theta a_0+O(h)
 \quad\hbox{in }H^3(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The factorization and its transpose give
\[
 L(T\boldsymbol z_h)=SM\boldsymbol z_h=0,\qquad M^T(K\boldsymbol z_h)=T^TL(T\boldsymbol z_h)=0.
\]
Thus the lift comes from an exact physical solution. The negative-phase
conjugation \eqref{eq:Kconjugated-minus} is
\[
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}Ke^{-\theta\cdot x/h}=-h^{-1}J_\theta+K_0.
\]
Substitution into \(y_h^F=-hK\boldsymbol z_h\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-fast-lift-expansion}
 \begin{aligned}
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}y_h^F
 ={}&J_\theta a_0+h(J_\theta a_1-K_0a_0)
       +J_\theta r_h\\
    &-h^2K_0a_1-hK_0r_h.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
On the fixed ball, the order-one operator \(J_\theta\) and order-two
operator \(K_0\) map the stated amplitude bounds into \(H^3\).
The remainder terms satisfy \(J_\theta r_h=O(h^2)\) and
\(hK_0r_h=O(h^3)\) there, proving the \(O(h)\) error.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:slow-physical-lift}
Under the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:fast-physical-lift}, assume in
addition that \(J_\theta a_0=0\).  Then
\[
 y_h^S=K\boldsymbol z_h
\]
satisfies
\[
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}y_h^S=K_0a_0-J_\theta a_1+O(h)
 \quad\hbox{in }H^3(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}).
\]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
With \(J_\theta a_0=0\), \eqref{eq:Kconjugated-minus} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-slow-lift-expansion}
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}y_h^S
 =K_0a_0-J_\theta a_1+hK_0a_1
       -h^{-1}J_\theta r_h+K_0r_h.
\end{equation}
All norms below are phase-removed norms on
\(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\). The operator orders and the stated bounds give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-slow-lift-error-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
 \|h^{-1}J_\theta r_h\|_{H^3}
 &\leq Ch^{-1}\|r_h\|_{H^5}=O(h),\\
 \|K_0r_h\|_{H^3}
 &\leq C\|r_h\|_{H^5}=O(h^2),\\
 \|hK_0a_1\|_{H^3}&=O(h).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The three bounds in \eqref{eq:proof-slow-lift-error-bounds} prove the limit and exhibit the $h^{-1}$ loss:
the reduced \(O(h^2)\) remainder yields an \(O(h)\) error for the unscaled adjoint lift in
\(H^3\).
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:audit-vii-low-regularity-moments}
The \(H^5\) reduced-remainder hypothesis above is stronger than needed
for the Betti volume limit. In the \(k=1\) case of
Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability}, the residual correction
\(r_h=-R_{h,\theta}^{(1)}(h\chi_0P_0a_1)\), for fixed smooth
amplitudes and cutoffs, satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:audit-vii-two-level-remainder}
 \|r_h\|_{H^1(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\leq Ch^2,
 \qquad \|r_h\|_{H^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\leq Ch.
\end{equation}
For this particular $L^2$ moment limit, it is enough that
$a_1\in H^3$ on a neighborhood of the fixed residual-cutoff support:
$P_0$ is of order two, so $\chi_0P_0a_1\in H^1$.  The source remains
compactly supported in the ball prescribed by
Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability}.  Applying that theorem to the compact
union of the two opposite normalized direction families gives the same
$h_0$ and uniform constants for both phase signs.  This does not
assert direction derivatives of the exact right inverses or a
global-in-space right-inverse equation.
Here \(K_0:H^2\to L^2\) and \(J_\theta:H^1\to L^2\)
are bounded on the fixed ball. When \(J_\theta a_0=0\), the exact
unscaled physical lift obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:audit-vii-slow-l2-bound}
\begin{aligned}
 &\left\|e^{\theta\cdot x/h}K\boldsymbol z_h
 -(K_0a_0-J_\theta a_1)\right\|_{L^2}\\
 &\hspace{1.5em}\leq C\left(h\|a_1\|_{H^2}
     +h^{-1}\|r_h\|_{H^1}+\|r_h\|_{H^2}\right)=O(h).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Similarly, the normalized principal physical lifts have \(O(h)\)
phase-removed errors in \(L^2\). For the first-order operator difference
\(D=M_1-M_2\) and a positive-phase primal CGO field
\(\boldsymbol z_{1,h}=e^{\theta\cdot x/h}(a+ha_1+r_{1,h})\), direct
conjugation gives
\[
 h e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}D\boldsymbol z_{1,h}
 =A_\theta a+h(A_\theta a_1+Da)+A_\theta r_{1,h}
    +h^2Da_1+hDr_{1,h}
 =A_\theta a+O_{L^2}(h).
\]
Consequently the nine normalized physical pairings converge with
\(O(h)\) error by Cauchy--Schwarz, provided that the opposite-phase
amplitudes are fixed and smooth on the working ball. Their exact
physical Betti identities are valid for each fixed \(h\); interior
elliptic regularity supplies smoothness near \(\overline{B_R}\).
Only the limiting quotient frame must be invertible for the ensuing
Plemelj argument: pointwise rank of the exact physical adjoint matrix
is not needed to establish the moments. The stronger \(H^5\) result
above remains useful for its \(H^3\) error bound and pointwise-rank
statement.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:CGO-uniformity}\label{rem:CGO-scope}
All applications use the fixed nested balls and source support of
Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability}, with the finite bounds of
Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo}. Physical lifts use the original
operators after undoing the background conjugation. Moment limits are
taken at fixed direction and fixed tests; parameter derivatives concern
only the smooth limiting frames and the reconstructed transport.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:CGO-product-limit}
Let \(D=A(x)\cdot\nabla+B(x)\) have smooth matrix coefficients
on a neighborhood of \(\overline{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}}\), where
\(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\subset\mathbb{R}^3\) is a fixed bounded domain.
Suppose that \(\theta\) ranges over a compact set and,
uniformly in the parameter family, that
\[
 \boldsymbol z_h=e^{\theta\cdot x/h}(a+h a_1+r_h),\qquad
 y_h=e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}(y+q_h),
\]
where \(a,a_1,y\) are bounded in \(H^3\),
\(\|r_h\|_{H^3}\leq Ch^2\), and \(\|q_h\|_{H^3}\leq Ch\).  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:CGO-product-limit}
 \left|h\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} y_h^T D\boldsymbol z_h\,dx-\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} y^T A_\theta a\,dx\right|\leq Ch,
 \qquad A_\theta:=\sum_{k=1}^3\theta_kA_k.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Conjugating \(D\) by the positive phase gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-product-conjugation}
 h e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}D\boldsymbol z_h
 =A_\theta a+h\widetilde e_h,
\end{equation}
where the full expansion is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-product-error-amplitude}
 \widetilde e_h=A_\theta a_1+Da+hDa_1
                 +h^{-1}A_\theta r_h+Dr_h.
\end{equation}
Indeed, we expand directly to obtain
\[
 h(A_\theta/h+D)(a+ha_1+r_h)
 =A_\theta a+hA_\theta a_1+A_\theta r_h
       +hDa+h^2Da_1+hDr_h.
\]
The coefficients are bounded on \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\), and
\(D:H^1(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})\to L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})\) is bounded. Applying these bounds to
\eqref{eq:proof-cgo-product-error-amplitude} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-product-error-bound}
\begin{aligned}
\|\widetilde e_h\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
\le C\bigl(
&\|a_1\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
+\|a\|_{H^1(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
+h\|a_1\|_{H^1(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\\
&+h^{-1}\|r_h\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
+\|r_h\|_{H^1(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
\bigr)
\le C.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Here we used \(\|r_h\|_{H^3(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\le Ch^2\) and the
stated bounds for the amplitudes. We use
\eqref{eq:proof-cgo-product-conjugation} and cancel the
exponential factors to obtain the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cgo-product-integral-error}
\begin{aligned}
h\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} y_h^T D \boldsymbol z_h\,dx
-\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} y^T A_\theta a\,dx
={}&\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} q_h^T A_\theta a\,dx\\
&+h\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} (y+q_h)^T\widetilde e_h\,dx.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:proof-cgo-product-integral-error},
\eqref{eq:proof-cgo-product-error-bound}, and the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\left|
h\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} y_h^T D \boldsymbol z_h\,dx
-\int_{\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}} y^T A_\theta a\,dx
\right|
\le{}&
\|q_h\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
\|A_\theta a\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\\
&+h\bigl(
\|y\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
+\|q_h\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
\bigr)
\|\widetilde e_h\|_{L^2(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
\le Ch.
\end{aligned}
\]
The constant is uniform on the stated compact parameter
family: the coefficients are fixed, the directions range
over a compact set, and all amplitude and remainder bounds
are uniform.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:matrix-dbar-frame}
Let \(m\) be a positive integer, let \(D\Subset\mathbb C\) be a disk, and let \(\mathcal B\)
be a smooth \(M_m(\mathbb C)\)-valued function on a neighborhood of
\(\overline D\). Then there is a smooth invertible matrix \(\Phi\)
on a neighborhood of \(\overline D\) satisfying
\begin{equation}\label{eq:matrix-dbar-frame}
 2\partial_{\bar z}\Phi=\mathcal B\Phi\quad\text{in }D.
\end{equation}
If \(\mathcal B(z,p)\) is jointly smooth on a fixed neighborhood of
\(\overline D\) for \(p\) in a finite-dimensional real parameter chart,
then, locally near each \(p_0\), the frame may be chosen jointly smooth
in \(z\) and \(p\). Its derivatives of every fixed finite order and
its inverse are bounded on compact spatial and parameter subcharts.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
We choose disks \(D\Subset D_1\Subset D_2\) inside the coefficient
neighborhood and shrink the parameter chart if needed so that these
disks are fixed for all nearby parameters.
For a fixed parameter \(p_0\), the operator
\(\bar\partial-\mathcal B_{p_0}d\bar z/2\) is integrable, since complex
dimension one has no nonzero \((0,2)\)-forms. The Koszul--Malgrange theorem
\cite{KoszulMalgrange1958} gives a holomorphic bundle structure. The underlying
smooth bundle on the contractible Stein disk \(D_2\) is trivial; the
Oka--Grauert principle \cite{Grauert1958} therefore makes it holomorphically
trivial. Thus it has a smooth invertible frame
\(\Phi_0\) solving \(\bar\partial\Phi_0=\mathcal B_{p_0}\Phi_0/2\).

Parameter dependence is obtained by the following local construction.
Choose \(\chi\in C_c^\infty(D_1)\) equal to one near \(\overline D\), and set
\[
 K_p=\tfrac12\chi\Phi_0^{-1}(\mathcal B_p-\mathcal B_{p_0})\Phi_0.
\]
The support of \(K_p\) lies in the fixed compact set
\(\operatorname{supp}\chi\Subset D_1\). Thus, for
\(u\in C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{D_1})\), the product \(K_pu\) extends
by zero to a compactly supported \(C^{0,\alpha}\) function on the plane.
Let \(\mathcal C_{\bar\partial}\) denote the planar Cauchy right inverse, normalized
by \(\bar\partial\mathcal C_{\bar\partial} f=f\). For $0<\alpha<1$ and
$f\in C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb C)$ supported in the fixed compact set
$\operatorname{supp}\chi\Subset D_1$, its standard Cauchy--Schauder bound is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:planar-dbar-Holder-bound}
 \|\mathcal C_{\bar\partial} f\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(\overline{D_1})}
 \leq C_{\alpha,D_1,\chi}\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb C)}.
\end{equation}

Consequently \(S_pu=\mathcal C_{\bar\partial}(K_pu)|_{D_1}\) is bounded on
\(C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{D_1})\), with norm tending to zero as
\(p\to p_0\).
On a sufficiently small parameter neighborhood the Neumann series defines
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-dbar-neumann-frame}
 \Psi_p=(I-S_p)^{-1}I_m,
 \qquad \|\Psi_p-I_m\|_{C^0(\overline{D_1})}<\tfrac12.
\end{equation}
In particular \(\Psi_p\) is invertible. Equation~\eqref{eq:proof-dbar-neumann-frame} gives
\(\bar\partial\Psi_p=K_p\Psi_p\), so \(\Phi_p=\Phi_0\Psi_p\) obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-dbar-cutoff-frame-equation}
 \bar\partial\Phi_p
 =\frac12\bigl[\mathcal B_{p_0}
       +\chi(\mathcal B_p-\mathcal B_{p_0})\bigr]\Phi_p.
\end{equation}
Where \(\chi=1\), \eqref{eq:proof-dbar-cutoff-frame-equation} is
\eqref{eq:matrix-dbar-frame}, including on a
neighborhood of \(\overline D\). Smooth dependence of the Banach-space
inverse in \eqref{eq:proof-dbar-neumann-frame} gives parameter dependence in \(C^{0,\alpha}\).
Equation~\eqref{eq:proof-dbar-neumann-frame}, Cauchy estimates, and parameter differentiation then give
all mixed spatial and parameter derivatives on the same parameter
neighborhood. Hence \(\Phi_p\) is jointly smooth. On compact parameter
subcharts, compactness
of \(\overline D\) supplies uniform bounds for each fixed derivative and
for \(\Phi_p^{-1}\).
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:graded-frame}
Suppose that the smooth strongly convex Lam\'e pair has a constant
exterior extension. Fix the nested CGO balls of
Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability} for its background-conjugated reduction,
write \(\Omega_0=B_{R_{\rm ext}}\), and choose \(L>R_{\rm ext}\).
Choose locally the orthonormal vectors \(e_1(\theta),e_2(\theta),
\nu_\theta\) and a spinor lift with \(\theta=\theta(s,t)\).
Around each normalized direction and transverse parameter there are
neighborhoods \(U,I\) on whose cylinder
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \mathcal C_{U,I,L}=\{(x,\theta):{}&
 x=x_\perp\nu_\theta+x_1e_1(\theta)+x_2e_2(\theta),\\
 &\theta\in U,\quad x_\perp\in I,\quad |x_1+\mathrm i x_2|<L\}
 \end{aligned}
\]
the two principal columns and the additional column give a jointly smooth
\(3\times3\) dual quotient frame \(Y\). It solves
\(2D_\theta Y+\Gamma_{\mu,\kappa}Y=0\) and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:graded-determinant}
 \det Y=-2c\det\Phi\det(n,\xi_q)=2c
\end{equation}
in the local coordinates and normalizations
\eqref{eq:fast-frame-normalization} and
\eqref{eq:slow-polarization-normalization}. The frame and its inverse
have bounded derivatives of every fixed finite order on compact
spatial and parameter subcharts.

Let \(I'\Subset I\) and choose \(\chi\in C_c^\infty(I)\) equal to
one near \(\overline{I'}\). There are exact physical solutions on
the fixed ball \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\) whose normalized canonical adjoint lifts form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Yh-matrix}
 \mathcal Y_h=(-hK\boldsymbol z_h^{F,1},-hK\boldsymbol z_h^{F,2},K\boldsymbol z_h^S),\qquad K=S^TT,
\end{equation}
and obey
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Yh-limit}
 e^{\theta\cdot x/h}\mathcal Y_h=W_\chi+E_h,
 \qquad \|E_h\|_{H^3(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}\leq Ch,
 \qquad W_\chi=\mathsf U_\theta Y\ \hbox{where }x_\perp\in I'.
\end{equation}
Here \(W_\chi\) is a smooth \(4\times3\) localized ambient amplitude
on \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\), of rank three on the set \(\chi=1\).
The constants are uniform on compact direction charts with the finite
amplitude and correction bounds of Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo}.
For any nonnegative integer \(r\), the analogous \(O(h)\) error
estimate in \(H^r\) follows from a reduced \(O(h^2)\) remainder
in \(H^{r+2}\) and the corresponding amplitude bounds.
The rank assertion uses the \(H^3\) estimate \eqref{eq:Yh-limit} and
Sobolev embedding. The parameter estimates concern the smooth
leading amplitudes and their transport corrections.
In particular, the unscaled third column uses the
$H^5$ bound for the reduced remainder in \eqref{eq:localized-cgo-remainder}:
the term $h^{-1}J_\theta r_h^S$ is $O(h)$ in $H^3$ by
\eqref{eq:proof-slow-lift-error-bounds}.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Choose the frames on a disk larger than \(\overline{D_L}\), where
\(D_L=\{z:|z|<L\}\), and shrink \(U,I\) so that the disks are fixed
after coordinate pullback. Lemma~\ref{lem:matrix-dbar-frame} and
\eqref{eq:Jfast}, \eqref{eq:slow-column-explicit} give smooth columns;
\eqref{eq:fast-dual-defect} and \eqref{eq:slow-dual-defect} prove their
dual transport.

Write \(\boldsymbol z_1^S=(v_S,f_S)\), \(R_S=c\theta\cdot v_S\), to distinguish
the first correction \(\boldsymbol z_1^S\) from the two columns of \(\Phi\). In quotient coordinates,
\eqref{eq:Qn} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-graded-frame-coordinates}
 Y=\begin{pmatrix}
   -2cf_1n&-2cf_2n&\xi_q+2cf_Sn\\
   -R_1&-R_2&R_S+\tfrac32c\,m\cdot q_\theta
 \end{pmatrix}.
\end{equation}
Expanding the two-row top block in the basis \((n,\xi_q)\) along
the third column's \(\xi_q\)-coordinate yields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-graded-determinant-expansion}
 \det Y=\det(n,\xi_q)\,[-2c(f_1R_2-f_2R_1)].
\end{equation}
The normalizations \(\det\Phi=1\), \(\det(n,\xi_q)=-1\) give
\eqref{eq:graded-determinant}.
Also \(\operatorname{tr}\Gamma=-2D_\theta\log c\), so \(2c\) satisfies determinant
transport. Since \(\min c>0\) on compact subcylinders, the adjugate
formula and joint smoothness give the finite inverse bounds.

Extend \(\chi(x_\perp)\boldsymbol z_0^{F,j}\) by zero in \(x_\perp\); homogeneous
transport is preserved because \(D_\theta\chi=0\). For the additional column set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:graded-slow-localization}
 a_{0,\chi}^S=\chi \boldsymbol z_0^S,\qquad
 a_{1,\chi}^S=\chi \boldsymbol z_1^S+w,\qquad
 (2D_\theta+A_\theta)w=[M,\chi]\boldsymbol z_0^S.
\end{equation}
The source \([M,\chi]\boldsymbol z_0^S=M(\chi \boldsymbol z_0^S)-\chi M\boldsymbol z_0^S\) is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-slow-localization-source}
 [M,\chi]\boldsymbol z_0^S=
 \binom{\chi''q_\theta-\chi'm(\nu_\theta\cdot q_\theta)}
       {d_{\mu,\kappa}\chi'(\nu_\theta\cdot q_\theta)}.
\end{equation}
Primes denote \(x_\perp\)-derivatives. Choose the ambient frame
\(C\) on the entire larger disk and a fixed leaf cutoff
\(\chi_{\rm leaf}\) inside its domain, equal to one near
\(\overline{D_L}\). The source need not be compact in the leaf variable;
its cutoff product defines
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-slow-localization-correction}
 w=\frac12C\mathcal C_\theta
       \bigl(\chi_{\rm leaf}C^{-1}[M,\chi]\boldsymbol z_0^S\bigr).
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:cauchy-leaf-estimate} solves the last equation of
\eqref{eq:graded-slow-localization} near \(\overline{D_L}\).
The integral acts at fixed \(x_\perp\), so \(w\) is supported in
\(\operatorname{supp}\chi'\cup\operatorname{supp}\chi''\Subset I\) and vanishes with all spatial
derivatives on each transverse neighborhood where \(\chi=1\).
Thus the amplitudes extend smoothly by zero, and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-localized-slow-transport}
 \begin{aligned}
 (2D_\theta+A_\theta)a_{1,\chi}^S
 &=\chi M\boldsymbol z_0^S+[M,\chi]\boldsymbol z_0^S=Ma_{0,\chi}^S,\\
 J_\theta a_{0,\chi}^S&=0.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
On transverse neighborhoods where \(\chi\) is identically one, the amplitudes equal \(\boldsymbol z_0^S,\boldsymbol z_1^S\). Since \(L>R_{\rm ext}\),
they and their transport equations are defined near the whole
\(\overline\Omega_0\) after transverse extension.

Apply Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo} with \(k=5\) to the conjugated
reduced amplitudes for the first two columns and to the prescribed pair
\(\mathcal R^{-1}a_{0,\chi}^S,\mathcal R^{-1}a_{1,\chi}^S\).
The covariance \eqref{eq:transport-covariance} and
\(\widetilde M=\mathcal R^{-1}M\mathcal R\) carry both transport
equations to these variables. Returning by \(\mathcal R\) preserves
the reduced \(O(h^2)\) remainder in \(H^5\) on the fixed ball.
Equation~\eqref{eq:factorization} gives exact physical fields
\(u_h^{F,j}=-hT\boldsymbol z_h^{F,j}\), \(u_h^S=T\boldsymbol z_h^S\), whose canonical lifts
are \eqref{eq:Yh-matrix}. Their leading amplitude on the entire working ball is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-localized-graded-amplitude}
 W_\chi=\bigl(\chi Y_F^{(1)},\ \chi Y_F^{(2)},
                K_0a_{0,\chi}^S-J_\theta a_{1,\chi}^S\bigr).
\end{equation}
On these transverse neighborhoods, the matrix is \(\mathsf U_\theta Y\):
\(a_{0,\chi}^S,a_{1,\chi}^S\) and all their spatial derivatives agree
with \(\boldsymbol z_0^S,\boldsymbol z_1^S\) there.
Lemmas~\ref{lem:fast-physical-lift} and \ref{lem:slow-physical-lift}
give \eqref{eq:Yh-limit} on the entire working ball.
Their expansions also give the \(H^r\) assertion from a reduced
\(H^{r+2}\) remainder and the corresponding amplitude bounds.
Uniformity uses the finite bounds of
Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo};
Proposition~\ref{prop:appendix-physical-realization} in
Appendix~\ref{app:cgo-input} provides the corresponding \(k=5\)
remainder bounds.

Fix \(K_{\rm pl}\) compactly inside the open transverse region on which \(\chi\) is identically one. The left inverse
\(\mathsf V_\theta^T\mathsf U_\theta=I_3\), Sobolev embedding,
and the inverse bounds give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-graded-rank-estimate}
 \left\|Y^{-1}\bigl(\mathsf V_\theta^T
       e^{\theta\cdot x/h}\mathcal Y_h-Y\bigr)\right\|_{C^0(K_{\rm pl})}
 \leq C_{\rm pl}h.
\end{equation}
For \(C_{\rm pl}h<1/2\), the projected matrix is invertible, hence
\(\mathcal Y_h\) has rank three on \(K_{\rm pl}\), uniformly on compact
direction subcharts.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:common-extension}
Let \(\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3\) be a bounded \(C^\infty\) domain and
let the smooth real Lam\'e pairs satisfy \eqref{eq:convexity}.
If their full DN maps agree on every component of \(\partial\Omega\),
then the pairs have equal boundary derivatives of every order. They admit smooth real
strongly convex extensions to \(\mathbb R^3\) which coincide outside
\(\Omega\) and equal one common constant pair outside a compact set.
In particular, for a sufficiently large enclosing ball
\(B_R=B(0,R)\) with \(\overline\Omega\Subset B_R\), these
extensions equal that constant pair near \(\partial B_R\).
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The equality of all boundary derivatives follows from
Theorem~\ref{thm:appendix-boundary-jets} in
Appendix~\ref{audrev-app-boundary-jets}, see
\eqref{audrev-boundary-all-jets}, for the outward DN convention
\eqref{eq:DN}. In each known boundary-normal chart and at every fixed
finite order, equal localized complete DN symbols give equal normal
traces of both Lam\'e parameters; tangential differentiation gives
all mixed boundary derivatives. The argument includes every boundary component and
the isotropic repeated stable root. Hence the coefficient difference
is flat on \(\partial\Omega\). The cited boundary determination works
\cite{NakamuraUhlmann1995,LinNakamura2017} provide the historical
context; the smooth isotropic conclusion required here follows from
the appendix's flux and symbol recursion.


Write \(a_j=(\lambda_j,\mu_j)\) and define \(d^0\) to equal
\(a_1-a_2\) on \(\overline\Omega\) and zero outside.
Boundary flatness makes \(d^0\) smooth on \(\mathbb R^3\).
Extend \(a_1\) smoothly through disjoint sufficiently thin collars
of all boundary components to a neighborhood of \(\overline\Omega\).
The uniform positive lower bounds in \eqref{eq:convexity} and
continuity allow the extension \(\widehat a_1\) to remain in the
open convex cone
\[
 \mathcal K_{\mathrm{ad}}=\{(\lambda,\mu)\in\mathbb R^2:
                   \mu>0,\ 3\lambda+2\mu>0\}.
\]
Set \(\widehat a_2=\widehat a_1-d^0\). It equals \(a_2\)
in \(\Omega\) and \(\widehat a_1\) on the exterior side of
every collar. Both extensions are smooth and strongly convex.

Choose a constant \(a_*\in\mathcal K_{\mathrm{ad}}\) and a cutoff
\(0\leq\chi\leq1\), equal to one near \(\overline\Omega\)
and supported in the neighborhood on which the collar extensions
are defined. Set
\[
 a_j^{\rm ext}=\chi\widehat a_j+(1-\chi)a_*
\]
there, and define \(a_j^{\rm ext}=a_*\) beyond the cutoff support,
including the remaining parts of bounded complementary components.
The definitions glue smoothly, and convexity of \(\mathcal K_{\mathrm{ad}}\)
preserves strong convexity. The pairs coincide outside \(\Omega\)
and are constant outside a compact set. A sufficiently large
origin-centered ball contains that set and \(\overline\Omega\)
strictly in its interior.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:enlargement}
For the common extensions of Lemma~\ref{lem:common-extension},
equality of the full DN maps on \(\partial\Omega\) implies
\[
 \Lambda_1^{B_R}=\Lambda_2^{B_R}.
\]
The complementary region \(B_R\setminus\overline\Omega\)
may be disconnected.
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Take real traces \(f\in H^{1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb R^3)\) and
\(g\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3)\), and set
\(A=B_R\setminus\overline\Omega\). We denote by \(u_{f,g}\) the
Dirichlet solution for the common medium on \(A\), with outer
trace \(f\) and interface trace \(g\) on every component of
\(\partial\Omega\). On a component of \(A\) that does not meet
\(\partial B_R\), all boundary values are prescribed by \(g\).
Strong convexity and Korn's inequality give coercivity on
\(H^1_0(A;\mathbb R^3)\), and hence existence and uniqueness componentwise.
Its summed energy is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-domain-annular-energy}
 \mathcal E_A(f,g)=\int_A
 \bigl[\lambda_A(\operatorname{div} u_{f,g})^2
       +2\mu_A\,\varepsilon(u_{f,g}):\varepsilon(u_{f,g})\bigr]\,dx,
\end{equation}
where \((\lambda_A,\mu_A)\) is the common extended pair on \(A\).
The Dirichlet principle gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-enlargement-dirichlet-minimum}
 \langle\Lambda_j^{B_R}f,f\rangle
 =\min_{g\in H^{1/2}(\partial\Omega;\mathbb R^3)}
       \bigl\{\mathcal E_A(f,g)
                 +\langle\Lambda_j^\Omega g,g\rangle\bigr\}.
\end{equation}
Indeed, restriction of a global competitor to \(\Omega\) and
all components of \(A\) gives a common interface trace and an
energy at least the sum of the corresponding Dirichlet minima.
Conversely, minimizers with matching trace \(g\) glue to an
\(H^1(B_R;\mathbb R^3)\) competitor with that summed energy. The
global Dirichlet minimizer exists by coercivity on
\(H^1_0(B_R;\mathbb R^3)\); its interface trace attains the minimum
in \eqref{eq:proof-enlargement-dirichlet-minimum}.
The common exterior energy and equality of the inner DN quadratic
forms therefore give equal outer quadratic forms for every real
\(f\). Bilinear polarization identifies the symmetric DN maps on
real traces, and their complex-linear extensions agree as well.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:uniform-leaf-disks}
Suppose that the two quotient connections agree outside
\(\overline{B_{R_0}}\), and choose radii
\(R_0<R_\perp<R_1<R\). Locally write a normalized null direction as
\(\theta=e_1+\mathrm i e_2\), where \((e_1,e_2,\nu_\theta)\) is an oriented
orthonormal triple, with \(\nu_\theta=e_1\times e_2\). Here \(x_\perp\) is the
real transverse coordinate. For \(|x_\perp|<R_\perp\), put
\[
 B_{\theta,x_\perp}=\{x_\perp\nu_\theta+x_1e_1+x_2e_2:
                    x_1^2+x_2^2<R_1^2-x_\perp^2\}.
\]
Each disk contains the coefficient-difference support on its leaf and has
a two-sided boundary annulus in the common exterior. The disks and annuli
vary smoothly in the local direction and transverse parameters. For
\(R_0<|x_\perp|<R_\perp\), the two connections agree on the entire leaf.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
On the leaf \(x_\perp=\nu_\theta\cdot x\), we use the complex coordinate
\(z=e_1\cdot x+\mathrm i e_2\cdot x\). The disk is
\(|z|<r(x_\perp)\), where \(r(x_\perp)=\sqrt{R_1^2-x_\perp^2}\). Its boundary lies on
\(\{|x|=R_1\}\), whereas the coefficient-difference support lies in
\(\{|x|\leq R_0\}\). Thus the disk contains all differences on its
leaf. Leaves with \(|x_\perp|>R_0\) miss their support entirely.

Choose \(\delta>0\) such that
\(\max(R_0,R_\perp)<R_1-\delta<R_1+\delta<R\). The intersection of the
leaf with \(R_1-\delta<|x|<R_1+\delta\) is the two-sided annulus
\[
 \sqrt{(R_1-\delta)^2-x_\perp^2}<|z|
                    <\sqrt{(R_1+\delta)^2-x_\perp^2}.
\]
Both radii are positive and smooth for \(|x_\perp|<R_\perp\), and the two
connections agree throughout this annulus. The disk pullback from
the unit disk is the complex-affine map \(w\mapsto r(x_\perp)w\).
On a compact transverse subinterval, the ratios of the annulus
radii to \(r(x_\perp)\) are uniformly separated from one. Hence one
fixed \(\varepsilon>0\) makes the image of
\(1-\varepsilon<|w|<1+\varepsilon\) lie in the annulus for all
parameters in that subchart. The local smooth orthonormal frames
then give the claimed joint smoothness in the direction as well.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:uniform-leafwise-frames}
Fix the nested balls used for the system CGO construction, with largest ball
\(\Omega_0=B_{R_{\rm ext}}\), and choose \(L>R_{\rm ext}\).
For every normalized null direction \(\theta_0\) and transverse parameter
\(x_{\perp,0}\in(-R_\perp,R_\perp)\), there are parameter neighborhoods \(U\) of
\(\theta_0\) and \(I\Subset(-R_\perp,R_\perp)\) of \(x_{\perp,0}\) on which the primal quotient frame
\(X_1\), a smooth ambient transport lift \(Z_1\), and the dual
quotient frame \(Y_2\) can be constructed on the whole cylinder
\[
 \{x=x_\perp\nu_\theta+x_1e_1(\theta)+x_2e_2(\theta):
             x_\perp\in I,\ |x_1+\mathrm i x_2|<L\}.
\]
The quotient frames have size \(3\times3\), and \(Z_1\) has size
\(4\times3\). They satisfy
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 (2D_\theta+\mathcal A_{1,\theta})X_1=0,\qquad
 (2D_\theta+A_{1,\theta})Z_1=0,\qquad
 \mathsf U_\theta^TZ_1=X_1,\\
 (2D_\theta+\Gamma_{2,\theta})Y_2=0.
 \end{gathered}
\]
All these objects are smooth jointly in the spatial and local direction
variables. On compact subcylinders and compact direction subcharts,
the quotient determinants are bounded away from zero, and every fixed
finite order of derivatives of the frames, their inverses, and the
ambient lift is bounded. In particular they extend across the closures
of the Plemelj disks and their exterior annuli.

For each fixed direction and every \(b\in C_c^\infty(I)\), the primal
leading amplitudes \(b(x_\perp)g(z)Z_1\), for holomorphic polynomials \(g\), have
exact CGO realizations throughout the physical ball. The columns of \(Y_2\)
have exact normalized physical dual realizations whose leading
ambient amplitude matrix is \(\mathsf U_\theta Y_2\) on a neighborhood
of \(\operatorname{supp}b\) in the transverse variable. The reduced CGO remainder may be taken \(O(h^2)\)
in \(H^5\); the resulting physical adjoint remainder is \(O(h)\) in \(H^3\).
The constants in the reduced $H^5$ remainder bound $O(h^2)$ and the normalized physical-adjoint $H^3$ remainder bound $O(h)$ may depend on the fixed direction, $b$, and $g$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
Choose a slightly larger planar disk than \(D_L=\{z:|z|<L\}\), where
\(z=e_1(\theta)\cdot x+\mathrm i e_2(\theta)\cdot x\). After pullback by the
smooth orthonormal coordinate map, every transport operator is a smooth
matrix \(\bar\partial\)-operator on this fixed disk, with \((\theta,x_\perp)\)
as parameters. The constructive parameter version in
Lemma~\ref{lem:matrix-dbar-frame} gives a primal quotient frame there.
Lemma~\ref{lem:transport-lift}, using a planar cutoff equal to one near
\(\overline{D_L}\), gives its ambient lift on the same cylinder.
For the dual frame we apply Theorem~\ref{thm:graded-frame} on the same
cylinder. In the local column normalization of \eqref{eq:graded-determinant},
\(\det Y_2=2\mu_2^{-1/2}\); hence its inverse is bounded on compact
subcylinders. Joint smoothness and compactness give the corresponding
bounds for both quotient frames and the ambient lift.

Since \(L>R_{\rm ext}\), multiplication by \(b(x_\perp)g(z)\) and zero
extension in \(x_\perp\) make the primal leading amplitude smooth near
\(\overline\Omega_0\). Its transport equation is preserved because
\(D_\theta b=D_\theta g=0\). Through the common background conjugation,
Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo} with direction \(-\theta\) supplies its
positive-phase realization. The covariance law
\eqref{eq:transport-covariance} and the bounded derivatives of
\(\mathcal R^{\pm1}\) preserve the transport equations and Sobolev
orders when returning to the original variables.

Choose \(\chi\in C_c^\infty(I)\) equal to one near \(\operatorname{supp} b\).
Theorem~\ref{thm:graded-frame}, with the commutator correction for the additional column
\eqref{eq:graded-slow-localization}, gives the negative-phase physical
dual realizations with leading matrix \(\mathsf U_\theta Y_2\) on this
transverse neighborhood. Both realizations are exact on \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\);
the reduced errors are \(O(h^2)\) in \(H^5\), and the normalized dual
errors are \(O(h)\) in \(H^3\). They may be restricted to \(B_R\).
Parameter smoothness concerns the limiting frames and amplitudes;
no parameter derivative of an exact CGO field is taken.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:sobolev-betti-dn}
For smooth real-valued Lam\'e coefficients strongly convex on
\(\overline{B_R}\), the DN map
\(H^{1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb C^3)\to H^{-1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb C^3)\)
is bounded and complex-bilinearly symmetric. More generally, if
\(u,v\in H^1(B_R;\mathbb C^3)\) and \(Lu,Lv\in L^2(B_R;\mathbb C^3)\)
in the distributional sense, their weak tractions \(t_u,t_v\)
belong to \(H^{-1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb C^3)\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-sobolev-betti}
 \int_{B_R}(u^TLv-v^TLu)\,dx
 =\langle t_v,\operatorname{Tr} u\rangle_{\partial B_R}
  -\langle t_u,\operatorname{Tr} v\rangle_{\partial B_R},
\end{equation}
where \(\operatorname{Tr}:H^1(B_R;\mathbb C^3)\to H^{1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb C^3)\)
is the trace. Boundary duality is written with the traction in
the first slot and is complex-bilinear. Thus the identity also
applies when only one field solves the homogeneous equation.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Define
\[
 a(u,w)=\int_{B_R}
 [\lambda(\operatorname{div} u)(\operatorname{div} w)+2\mu\,\varepsilon(u):\varepsilon(w)]\,dx.
\]
Using strong convexity and Korn's inequality, we obtain, for some \(c_0>0\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-betti-coercivity}
 a(w,\overline w)\geq c_0\|w\|_{H^1}^2,
 \qquad w\in H^1_0(B_R;\mathbb C^3).
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:proof-betti-coercivity} and a bounded trace right inverse \(E\),
we obtain the Dirichlet estimate
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-betti-dirichlet-estimate}
 \|u_f\|_{H^1}\leq C\|f\|_{H^{1/2}}.
\end{equation}
For \(u\in H^1\) with \(Lu\in L^2\), we define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-weak-traction-definition}
 \langle t_u,g\rangle
 =a(u,W)+\int_{B_R}(Lu)^TW\,dx,\qquad \operatorname{Tr} W=g.
\end{equation}
The distributional equation makes the definition
\eqref{eq:proof-weak-traction-definition} independent of the lifting:
the difference of two liftings has zero trace and its contribution
vanishes by density in \(H^1_0\). Taking \(W=Eg\) in
\eqref{eq:proof-weak-traction-definition}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-betti-traction-estimate}
 \|t_u\|_{H^{-1/2}}\leq C(\|u\|_{H^1}+\|Lu\|_{L^2}).
\end{equation}
For smooth fields, \(t_u\) is the outward traction \(\sigma(u)\nu_{\partial B_R}\).
In \eqref{eq:proof-weak-traction-definition}, we use \(u\) as a lifting
for \(t_v\), and \(v\) for \(t_u\). By subtraction and the symmetry
of \(a\), we obtain \eqref{eq:proof-sobolev-betti}. 
For homogeneous Dirichlet solutions, \(t_{u_f}=\Lambda f\).
The estimates \eqref{eq:proof-betti-dirichlet-estimate} and
\eqref{eq:proof-betti-traction-estimate}, with \(Lu_f=0\), give the
boundedness of \(\Lambda\); applying \eqref{eq:proof-sobolev-betti} to two
homogeneous solutions gives its complex-bilinear symmetry.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:primal-adjoint}
Assume that the two Lam\'e pairs coincide on a neighborhood of
\(\partial B_R\) and that \(\Lambda_1=\Lambda_2\) as bounded operators
\(H^{1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb C^3)\to H^{-1/2}(\partial B_R;\mathbb C^3)\).
Let \(M_1\boldsymbol z_1=0\), set \(u_1=T_1\boldsymbol z_1\), and let \(u_2\) solve
\(L_2u_2=0\). Assume that \(\boldsymbol z_1\) and \(u_2\) are smooth near
\(\overline{B_R}\), as holds for the CGO constructions on a larger ball.
Put \(y_2=S_2^Tu_2\). All volume integrals in this proposition are
over \(B_R\). Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:primal-adjoint}
 \int y_2^T(M_1-M_2)\boldsymbol z_1\,dx=0.
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The factorization \eqref{eq:factorization} and its bilinear transpose give
\[
 L_1u_1=S_1M_1\boldsymbol z_1=0,\qquad
 M_2^Ty_2=M_2^TS_2^Tu_2=T_2^TL_2u_2=0.
\]
Write \(w=M_2\boldsymbol z_1=(w',w_4)^T\), with \(w'\in\mathbb C^3\).
In the common boundary collar, \(M_2=M_1\), so \(w=0\). Thus
\[
 \int y_2^T(M_1-M_2)\boldsymbol z_1\,dx
 =-\int (S_2^Tu_2)^Tw\,dx.
\]
Using \eqref{eq:Stranspose}, integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-adjoint-transfer}
 \int (S_2^Tu_2)^Tw\,dx
 =\int u_2^TS_2w\,dx
  -\int_{\partial B_R}(u_2\cdot\nu_{\partial B_R})
                       \frac{w_4}{1-\kappa_2}\,dS.
\end{equation}
Since \(w_4=0\) in the collar, the boundary term vanishes, and
\eqref{eq:factorization} for the second medium gives
\[
 \int y_2^T(M_1-M_2)\boldsymbol z_1\,dx
 =-\int u_2^TS_2M_2\boldsymbol z_1\,dx
 =-\int u_2^TL_2v\,dx,\qquad v=T_2\boldsymbol z_1.
\]

The smooth fields \(u_2,v\) satisfy Lemma~\ref{lem:sobolev-betti-dn}.
Set \(f=\operatorname{Tr} u_1\) and \(g=\operatorname{Tr} u_2\). In the common collar,
\(T_2=T_1\) and \(v=u_1\); equality of the coefficients and fields gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr} v=f,\qquad
 t_v^{(2)}=t_{u_1}^{(1)}=\Lambda_1f,\qquad
 t_{u_2}^{(2)}=\Lambda_2g.
\]
The traction superscript specifies its medium. The comparison field
\(v\) need not satisfy \(L_2v=0\): \(L_2v\in L^2\) and \(L_2u_2=0\)
suffice for Betti's identity \eqref{eq:proof-sobolev-betti}, which gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 -\int u_2^TL_2v\,dx
 &=-\langle\Lambda_1f,g\rangle
       +\langle\Lambda_2g,f\rangle\\
 &=\langle(\Lambda_2-\Lambda_1)f,g\rangle=0,
 \end{aligned}
\]
by bilinear symmetry of \(\Lambda_2\) and equality of the DN maps.
These Sobolev duality pairings satisfy
\[
 |\langle\Lambda_jf,g\rangle|
 \leq\|\Lambda_j\|_{H^{1/2}\to H^{-1/2}}
       \|f\|_{H^{1/2}}\|g\|_{H^{1/2}}.
\]

Under the collar hypotheses, every exact physical pair satisfies
\eqref{eq:primal-adjoint}. Each fixed-\(h\) CGO pairing is therefore
zero before phase cancellation. The limit uses phase-removed interior
bounds, without uniform bounds on exponentially weighted boundary traces.


For the common background conjugation, set
\[
 \widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_1=\mathcal R^{-1}\boldsymbol z_1,\qquad
 \widetilde y_2=\mathcal R^Ty_2,\qquad
 \widetilde M_j=\mathcal R^{-1}M_j\mathcal R.
\]
The identity of differential operators
\(\widetilde M_1-\widetilde M_2
  =\mathcal R^{-1}(M_1-M_2)\mathcal R\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-exact-moment-covariance}
 \int\widetilde y_2^T(\widetilde M_1-\widetilde M_2)
                         \widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_1\,dx
 =\int y_2^T(M_1-M_2)\boldsymbol z_1\,dx=0.
\end{equation}
This exact covariance includes the zeroth-order terms and derivatives
of \(\mathcal R\); \eqref{eq:moment-covariance} gives its leading
first-order counterpart.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:DN-to-moment}
Let \(D=\sum_k A^k\partial_k+B\), \(\boldsymbol z_h\), and \(y_h\) satisfy
Lemma~\ref{lem:CGO-product-limit} on \(B_R\), with the coefficients of
\(D\) compactly supported there. Its \(O(h)\) estimate holds on \(B_R\),
and both integrals are unchanged by enlarging the integration domain
through a region where the coefficients of \(D\) vanish, with the fields
held fixed.
If \(D=M_1-M_2\), \(M_1\boldsymbol z_h=0\), and \(y_h=S_2^Tu_{2,h}\) with
\(L_2u_{2,h}=0\), under the common-collar and exact-solution hypotheses
of Proposition~\ref{prop:primal-adjoint}, equality of the elastic DN maps
makes the exact integral zero and hence
\[
 \int_{B_R}y^T A_\theta a\,dx=0.
\]
The assertion applies to every pair of columns of matrix-valued fields.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
In Lemma~\ref{lem:CGO-product-limit}, the exponential phases cancel in
the volume pairing before the limit is taken. For a physical adjoint
field, Proposition~\ref{prop:primal-adjoint} sets that exact pairing
to zero at each fixed \(h\). Its \(O(h)\) interior error therefore
bounds the leading moment. Vanishing of the coefficients of \(D\)
proves the integration-domain assertion, and applying the scalar
pairing to each pair of columns proves the matrix assertion.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:physical-nine-moments}\label{prop:quotient-moment}
Let the two smooth strongly convex Lam\'e pairs have the common
exterior extensions and fixed CGO domains chosen above, with their
coefficient difference supported in \(\overline{B_{R_0}}\), and assume
that their physical DN maps on \(B_R\) agree. Fix a normalized null
direction and one of the local transverse intervals
\(I\Subset(-R_\perp,R_\perp)\) of
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-leafwise-frames}.

There are invertible primal and dual quotient frames \(X_1,Y_2\)
on the ambient cylinder, chosen before any test functions, such that
the dual frame consists of two principal and one additional canonical physical
adjoint polarizations. In the normalizations of
Theorem~\ref{thm:graded-frame},
\[
 \det Y_2=2\mu_2^{-1/2}\ne0.
\]
For every fixed \(b\in C_c^\infty(I)\) and holomorphic polynomial
\(g\), these frames have exact realizations on the fixed CGO ball:
the primal reduced fields solve \(M_1\boldsymbol z_{1,h}=0\), and every dual
column is \(S_2^Tu_{2,h}\) with \(L_2u_{2,h}=0\). Their reduced
remainders are \(O(h^2)\) in \(H^5\), and the normalized physical adjoint
remainders are \(O(h)\) in \(H^3\). On a transverse neighborhood of
\(\operatorname{supp} b\), the leading primal and dual amplitudes are respectively
\(b(x_\perp)g(z)Z_1\) and \(\mathsf U_\theta Y_2\), with
\(\mathsf U_\theta^TZ_1=X_1\). The exact physical adjoint matrix has
rank three on compact subsets of this region for sufficiently small
\(h\).

The exact primal and three physical-adjoint CGO realizations give all entries of the matrix identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:3Dmoment}
 \int b(x_\perp)g(z)Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dx=0.
\end{equation}
The integral is over \(B_R\), with zero extension outside the
transverse support of \(b\). On every enclosing disk
\(B=B_{\theta,x_\perp}\), \(x_\perp\in I\), they also give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:leaf-moment}
 \int_B g(z)Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dA=0.
\end{equation}
Both are full \(3\times3\) matrix identities for the same quotient
frames and all holomorphic polynomials. They give nine scalar identities
for each polynomial weight, rather than only finitely many weighted
moment conditions. The leafwise identity holds
at every transverse parameter. The only boundary map used is the
physical elasticity DN map.
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{thm:graded-frame} supplies canonical physical adjoint
columns: the principal pair spans \(\mathcal F_\theta\), and
\eqref{eq:Yslow-mod} and \eqref{eq:slow-polarization-normalization} make
the additional column independent, with determinant
\eqref{eq:graded-determinant}. Together with
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-leafwise-frames}, it gives \(X_1,Y_2,Z_1\)
on the cylinder, fixed before \(b,g\). The principal columns give the
first two rows and the additional column the third row of
\(Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\); all nine entries use these same frames.
The rank of the exact fields follows from \eqref{eq:Yh-limit}, the bounded
inverse of \(Y_2\), and Sobolev embedding, as in
\eqref{eq:proof-graded-rank-estimate}.
\space{}For fixed \(\theta,b,g\), Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-leafwise-frames}
gives the \(4\times3\) amplitude \(a=b(x_\perp)g(z)Z_1\), smoothly extended
by zero in \(x_\perp\) near the full fixed CGO domain, with
\((2D_\theta+A_{1,\theta})a=0\). Apply
Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo} columnwise through the common
background conjugation with direction \(-\theta\). Its leading
transport is the negative of this homogeneous equation, and its
first correction in the original variables satisfies
\[
 (2D_\theta+A_{1,\theta})a_1=-M_1a
 \quad\hbox{near }\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},
\]
The exact primal fields, with their two-order remainder controlled by
Proposition~\ref{prop:appendix-physical-realization}, are
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-moment-primal-realization}
 \begin{gathered}
 \boldsymbol z_{1,h}=e^{\theta\cdot x/h}(a+h a_1+r_h),
 \qquad M_1\boldsymbol z_{1,h}=0\quad\hbox{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},\\
 \|r_h\|_{H^5(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}=O(h^2).
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
The fixed CGO ball \(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\) contains \(\overline{B_R}\).

Choose a transverse cutoff equal to one near \(\operatorname{supp} b\).
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-leafwise-frames} realizes the fixed dual frame
as a \(4\times3\) matrix of exact physical adjoint fields
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-moment-dual-realization}
 y_{2,h}=e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}(\widehat y_2+q_h),
 \qquad \|q_h\|_{H^3(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}=O(h),
\end{equation}
Here \(\widehat y_2=\mathsf U_\theta Y_2\) near \(\operatorname{supp} b\).
Every column is exactly \(S_2^Tu_{2,h}\), with \(L_2u_{2,h}=0\):
