the two normalized physical fields are \(-hT_2\boldsymbol z_h^{F,j}\), and the unscaled field
is \(T_2\boldsymbol z_h^S\). The physical-adjoint column normalizations $(-h,-h,1)$ preserve
\eqref{eq:primal-adjoint}. The cutoff and corrections may depend
on \(b\), while their leading columns on its support stay fixed.
Lemma~\ref{lem:slow-physical-lift} already accounts for the additional
lift's \(h^{-1}\) factor and gives its unscaled \(O(h)\) conjugated
\(H^3\) error. Outside the open region where the cutoff is identically one \(a=0\), so the leading product
vanishes.


The phase-removed principal adjoint lifts
\(K_2\boldsymbol z_h^{F,j}\), \(j=1,2\), are of order
\(O(h^{-1})\), whereas the supplementary lift
\(K_2\boldsymbol z_h^S\) is of order \(O(1)\).
Since the conjugated first-order operator difference
contributes a factor \(h^{-1}\), the unnormalized
pairings have respective orders
\(O(h^{-2}),O(h^{-2}),O(h^{-1})\).
Combining the physical normalization factors
\((-h,-h,1)\) with the common factor \(h\) in
\eqref{eq:proof-matrix-moment-limit} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:physical-moment-row-normalization}
 h\,\operatorname{diag}(-h,-h,1)
 =\operatorname{diag}(-h^2,-h^2,h).
\end{equation}
All three rows thus admit finite limits, while the
rescaled Betti identities remain exactly zero for
every \(h>0\).

For each fixed direction, the primal and adjoint frames
are chosen independently of the test functions \(b\) and
\(g\). The limit \(h\to0\) is taken for each fixed test
pair, yielding the matrix identities for all such tests
without requiring estimates uniform in the polynomial
degree or direction derivatives of the exact fields.
Only the invertibility of the frames is needed; no
nondegeneracy assumption on the coefficient difference
is imposed.





In particular, the third row is obtained from the first
nonzero physical lift \(K_0\boldsymbol z_0^S-J_\theta \boldsymbol z_1^S\), not by prescribing an
independent row of auxiliary Cauchy data. The same invertible \(Y_2\)
is used for all three primal columns and for every fixed polynomial
test. Each entry therefore follows from the exact elasticity Betti
identity \eqref{eq:primal-adjoint}, together with the whole-domain
\(O(h)\) limit in \eqref{eq:proof-matrix-moment-limit}.

All fields are smooth near \(\overline{B_R}\). In the original
variables, the operator difference is
\[
 M_1-M_2=\sum_{k=1}^3(A_{1,k}-A_{2,k})\partial_k+(B_1-B_2),
\]
Its coefficients are compactly supported in \(B_R\). The remainder
estimates \eqref{eq:proof-moment-primal-realization} and
\eqref{eq:proof-moment-dual-realization}, restricted to \(B_R\),
and the fixed \(H^3\) amplitude bounds satisfy
Corollary~\ref{lem:DN-to-moment}. The error estimate \eqref{eq:CGO-product-limit}
controls corrections on the whole integration domain,
including where \(a=0\). Applied to all nine column pairs, the
corollary gives, in a fixed matrix norm,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-matrix-moment-limit}
 \begin{aligned}
 \Big\|{}&h\int_{B_R}y_{2,h}^T(M_1-M_2)\boldsymbol z_{1,h}\,dx\\
 &-\int_{B_R}b(x_\perp)g(z)Y_2^T\mathsf U_\theta^T
       (A_{1,\theta}-A_{2,\theta})Z_1\,dx\Big\|\leq Ch.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
The first integral is zero by \eqref{eq:primal-adjoint}, so the
second vanishes as \(h\to0\). Write
\(Z_1=\mathsf V_\theta X_1+e_\theta\alpha\). The common quotient
basis, \((A_{1,\theta}-A_{2,\theta})e_\theta=0\), and
\eqref{eq:primal-quotient-connection} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-moment-quotient-contraction}
 \mathsf U_\theta^T(A_{1,\theta}-A_{2,\theta})Z_1
 =(\mathcal A_{1,\theta}-\mathcal A_{2,\theta})X_1
 =2(P_1-P_2)X_1.
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:proof-matrix-moment-limit} and
\eqref{eq:proof-moment-quotient-contraction} give
\[
 0=\int_{B_R}b(x_\perp)g(z)Y_2^T
       (\mathcal A_{1,\theta}-\mathcal A_{2,\theta})X_1\,dx
   =2\int_{B_R}b(x_\perp)g(z)Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dx,
\]
proving \eqref{eq:3Dmoment}. 
The common background conjugation preserves the leading moment by
\eqref{eq:moment-covariance}.


Fix a polynomial \(g\) and the enclosing disks of
Lemma~\ref{lem:uniform-leaf-disks}.
Identify \(B_{x_\perp}=\{z:|z|<r(x_\perp)\}\), where
\(r(x_\perp)=\sqrt{R_1^2-x_\perp^2}\), with the unit disk \(\mathbb D\)
by \(\Phi_{x_\perp}(w)=r(x_\perp)w\).
Set
\[
\begin{aligned}
F_g(x_\perp,z)&=g(z)Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1,\\
H(x_\perp)&=\int_{B_{x_\perp}}F_g(x_\perp,z)\,dA(z).
\end{aligned}
\]
The pullback \(\widehat F_{g,x_\perp}(w)=r(x_\perp)^2F_g(x_\perp,r(x_\perp)w)\)
is jointly smooth on \(J\times\overline{\mathbb D}\) for every
\(J\Subset I\); hence \(H\) is continuous on \(I\).
Each disk contains the leafwise difference support. Using
\(dx=dA\,d x_\perp\), \eqref{eq:3Dmoment} gives
\(\int_I b(x_\perp)H(x_\perp)\,d x_\perp=0\) for every \(b\in C_c^\infty(I)\).
Entrywise distributional vanishing and continuity imply
\(H(x_\perp)=0\) for every \(x_\perp\in I\).

This proves \eqref{eq:leaf-moment} at every \(x_\perp\in I\).
The local intervals cover all enclosing disks, and the integrand
vanishes on leaves outside the difference support.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:ER-leading-and-slow}
The identity \(H_2=0\) in
\cite[Section~3, equations (3.4)--(3.5) and the paragraph following (3.5)]{EskinRalstonSystems2004}
depends on the two contracted leading reduced amplitudes
\((\theta\cdot r_0,s_0)\), where \(r_0,s_0\) are the vector and
scalar amplitudes in that reference. The relation obtained after
its equation (3.5) is equivalent to five scalar equations and,
by itself, admits nonconstant candidate coefficients even when
the first pair is constant.


The same work also derives the full sequence of asymptotic
invariants and uses \(H_0=0\) in its small-gradient argument
\cite[Section~3]{EskinRalstonSystems2004}; see also
\cite[Section~5]{EskinRalston2002}.
Here higher-order terms in canonical physical adjoint lifts are
organized into the complete weighted quotient pairing.
The two principal columns \eqref{eq:Jfast} span \(\mathcal F_\theta\)
and produce the first two rows for the ordered frame of
Theorem~\ref{thm:physical-nine-moments}. When
\(J_\theta \boldsymbol z_0^S=0\), the unscaled lift retains
\(Y_S=K_0\boldsymbol z_0^S-J_\theta \boldsymbol z_1^S\); its class
\eqref{eq:Yslow-mod} lies outside \(\mathcal F_\theta\).


Write \(Y_2=(y_{F,1},y_{F,2},y_S)\) in quotient coordinates,
so that the ambient additional amplitude is \(\mathsf U_\theta y_S\).
For the standard basis vectors \(\mathsf e_k\) of \(\mathbb C^3\),
the additional polarization supplies the three additional identities

\begin{equation}\label{eq:slow-third-row-moments}
 \int_B g(z)y_S^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\mathsf e_k\,dA=0,
 \qquad k=1,2,3.
\end{equation}


Combining the third-row identities in
\eqref{eq:slow-third-row-moments} with the two principal
rows yields the full matrix identity \eqref{eq:leaf-moment},
with both transport weights retained.
The derivation relies on the exact primal--physical-adjoint
identity \eqref{eq:primal-adjoint} and the physical elastic
Dirichlet-to-Neumann maps.
The resulting complete matrix identities provide the contour
compatibility required for the subsequent Cauchy--Plemelj
construction.



The additional row completes the two-dimensional image of the leading physical adjoint lift
in this fixed-frame formulation. All nine scalar entries of the matrix moment identity \eqref{eq:leaf-moment} come from the same physical elasticity DN map. The comparison made here is
with the contracted \(H_2\) identity; the argument does not require
a term-by-term identification with the full asymptotic sequence
\(\{H_j\}\), or independence from that sequence.
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:cauchy-green-moment}
Let \(B\subset\mathbb C\) be a disk and let
\(\mathsf W\in C^1(\overline B;M_m(\mathbb C))\). Let \(X_1,Y_2,P_1,P_2\) be
smooth \(m\times m\) matrix fields near \(\overline B\). If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cauchy-green-source}
 2\partial_{\bar z}\mathsf W=-Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-cauchy-green-volume-moments}
 \int_B g(z)Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dA=0
\end{equation}
for every holomorphic polynomial \(g\), then
\[
 \oint_{\partial B}g(z)\mathsf W(z)\,dz=0
 \qquad\text{for every holomorphic polynomial }g.
\]
The assertion is entrywise and remains valid for jointly smooth
parameter families after complex-affine pullback to a fixed disk.
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
With the stated orientation, the Cauchy--Green formula and
\eqref{eq:proof-cauchy-green-source}--\eqref{eq:proof-cauchy-green-volume-moments}
give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \oint_{\partial B}g\mathsf W\,dz
 &=2\mathrm i\int_B\partial_{\bar z}(g\mathsf W)\,dA\\
 &=-\mathrm i\int_BgY_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dA=0.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here \(\partial_{\bar z}g=0\), and all identities hold entrywise.
For \(\Phi_p(w)=a_pw+b_p\), the contour integral becomes
\[
 a_p\oint_{\partial\mathbb D}
       g(\Phi_p(w))\mathsf W_p(\Phi_p(w))\,dw.
\]
Thus the contour differential contributes \(a_p\), whereas the
area differential contributes \(|a_p|^2\). On compact parameter
subcharts the pulled-back integrand and all its parameter derivatives
are continuous on a fixed circle, so differentiation under that
integral is justified.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:matrix-plemelj}
Let \(B\subset\mathbb C\) be a disk and let
\(\mathsf W\in C^\infty(\overline B;\mathrm{GL}(m,\mathbb C))\). If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-plemelj-contour-moments}
 \oint_{\partial B}z^n\mathsf W(z)\,dz=0\qquad(n=0,1,2,\ldots),
\end{equation}
then the Cauchy factor
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-plemelj-cauchy-factor}
 \mathsf R(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm i}\oint_{\partial B}
              \frac{\mathsf W(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta,\qquad z\in B,
\end{equation}
is holomorphic in \(B\), extends smoothly and invertibly to
\(\overline B\), and has boundary trace \(\mathsf W|_{\partial B}\).

Moreover, in the Euclidean matrix operator norm,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:plemelj-maximum-inverse-bound}
 \sup_{z\in\overline B}\|\mathsf R(z)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}
 \leq \sup_{z\in\partial B}\|\mathsf W(z)^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}},
 \qquad
 \min_{z\in\overline B}|\det\mathsf R(z)|
 \geq\min_{z\in\partial B}|\det\mathsf W(z)|>0.
\end{equation}

For a jointly smooth local family under smooth complex-affine
pullbacks from a fixed disk, \(\mathsf R\) and \(\mathsf R^{-1}\) are jointly smooth
on the pulled-back closed disks. Their derivatives of each fixed
finite order are bounded on compact parameter subcharts.
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
For \(|z|\) larger than every \(|\zeta|\) on \(\partial B\), we expand
\((\zeta-z)^{-1}=-\sum_{n\geq0}\zeta^nz^{-n-1}\). The moments
\eqref{eq:proof-plemelj-contour-moments} make the exterior Cauchy
transform zero there, hence throughout the connected exterior.
The Plemelj jump formula therefore gives
\(\mathsf R|_{\partial B}=\mathsf W|_{\partial B}\).

For the parameter assertion, we denote the smooth matrix family by
\(\mathsf W_p\) and its complex-affine pullbacks from the unit disk by \(\Phi_p\).
Set \(\mathsf W_p^\sharp=\mathsf W_p\circ\Phi_p\) and denote by \(\mathsf R_p\)
its Cauchy factor on that disk. An affine change of contour
preserves polynomial moments, so the negative Fourier modes vanish.
The remaining coefficients and Cauchy factor are

\begin{equation}\label{eq:plemelj-fourier-series}
 \mathsf W_{n,p}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_0^{2\pi}
       \mathsf W_p^\sharp(e^{it})e^{-int}\,dt,\qquad
 \mathsf R_p(w)=\sum_{n\geq0}\mathsf W_{n,p}w^n.
\end{equation}

Repeated integration by parts gives, for every \(N\) and parameter
multi-index \(\alpha\), uniformly on compact parameter subcharts,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-plemelj-fourier-decay}
 \|\partial_p^\alpha\mathsf W_{n,p}\|
       \le C_{\alpha,N}(1+n)^{-N},\qquad n\geq0.
\end{equation}
A spatial derivative of order \(r\) contributes at most
\(C_r(1+n)^r\); taking \(N>r+1\) makes every fixed mixed spatial
and parameter derivative uniformly summable on the closed disk.
Thus \(\mathsf R_p\) is jointly smooth up to the contour, also for a fixed
matrix viewed as a constant parameter family.

The nonvanishing continuous function \(\det\mathsf W\) on
\(\overline B\) contracts its boundary loop in \(\mathbb C^*\), so its
winding number is zero. The boundary trace gives the same loop for
\(\det \mathsf R\). Interior circles sufficiently close to \(\partial B\)
have that winding number because the boundary determinant is
nonzero. The argument principle therefore excludes interior zeros
of \(\det \mathsf R\), proving invertibility on the closed disk.


For fixed Euclidean unit vectors \(u,v\in\mathbb C^m\), the scalar function
\(z\mapsto v^*\mathsf R(z)^{-1}u\) is holomorphic in \(B\).
The maximum-modulus principle and the equality
\(\mathsf R=\mathsf W\) on \(\partial B\) imply
\[
 |v^*\mathsf R(z)^{-1}u|
 \leq \sup_{\partial B}\|\mathsf W^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
\]
Taking the supremum over \(u,v\) proves the first estimate in
\eqref{eq:plemelj-maximum-inverse-bound}. The minimum determinant
bound follows by applying the same principle to the holomorphic
function \(1/\det\mathsf R\). Both inequalities apply pointwise
in every smooth parameter family, giving parameter-uniform inverse
bounds whenever the boundary inverse-frame bounds are uniform.


In a compact parameter subchart, continuity and nonvanishing of
\(\det \mathsf R_p\) on the product with the closed reference disk give a
uniform positive lower bound. The adjugate formula then gives joint
smoothness and every stated finite derivative bound for \(\mathsf R_p^{-1}\).
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:Gunique}
Let \(P_1,P_2\) be smooth square matrix coefficients on a connected
open leaf domain. Every smooth matrix \(W\) satisfying
\[
 2\partial_{\bar z}W+P_1W-WP_2=0
\]
and vanishing on a nonempty open subset vanishes everywhere.
Consequently two relative transports with the same equation and
the same values on a nonempty open subset coincide. In particular,
identity-normalized transports coincide on each connected leaf
overlap meeting their common normalization set.
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
On a small disk we choose invertible frames \(X_j\) satisfying
\(2\partial_{\bar z}X_j+P_jX_j=0\), by
Lemma~\ref{lem:matrix-dbar-frame}. We differentiate their inverses to obtain
\[
 2\partial_{\bar z}(X_1^{-1}WX_2)
 =X_1^{-1}(2\partial_{\bar z}W+P_1W-WP_2)X_2=0.
\]
The transformed matrix is holomorphic. If it vanishes on a
nonempty open part of that disk, the identity theorem makes it
zero on the whole disk. A chain of overlapping disks along a path
in the connected leaf domain propagates this conclusion to every
point. We apply the result to the difference of two transports.
Only local frames are used, including on a multiply connected annulus.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-16: body_md -->
\label{lem:distributional-annulus-gluing}
Let \(B\subset\mathbb C\) be a disk and let \(A\) be a connected two-sided
annulus of \(\partial B\). Let \(P_1,P_2\) be smooth matrix
coefficients near \(\overline B\) and on \(A\), with \(P_1=P_2\)
on \(A\). Suppose that
\(G_{\mathrm{in}}\in C^\infty(B)\cap C^0(\overline B)\) solves
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-annulus-interior-transport}
 2\partial_{\bar z}G_{\mathrm{in}}+P_1G_{\mathrm{in}}-G_{\mathrm{in}}P_2=0
\end{equation}
in \(B\), and that \(G_{\mathrm{in}}|_{\partial B}=I\). Define
\[
 G(z)=
 \begin{cases}
 G_{\mathrm{in}}(z),&z\in B,\\
 I,&z\in A\setminus\overline B\text{ or }z\in\partial B.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then \(G\) is a distributional solution of the relative equation
on \(B\cup A\), is smooth across the contour, and equals \(I\)
throughout \(A\), including its inner part.
<!-- end lemma-16 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Continuity on \(\overline B\) and \eqref{eq:proof-annulus-interior-transport}
make
\(\partial_{\bar z}G_{\mathrm{in}}\) bounded near the circle.
Apply integration by parts with a compactly supported smooth test
function on concentric interior disks and let their radii approach
the boundary radius. The traces converge uniformly, and the bounded
source permits passing to the limit in the volume integral. On
the exterior part of \(A\), the inner boundary is oriented oppositely.
The two boundary contributions therefore cancel because the inside
and outside traces both equal \(I\). Equivalently, the contour
measure in the distributional derivative is proportional to their
zero trace jump. Since \(P_1=P_2\) on \(A\), the outside field \(I\)
also satisfies the relative equation. This proves the distributional
equation on \(B\cup A\).

We choose a small disk contained in \(A\) and crossing the circle.
An invertible local frame \(X\) for the common coefficient \(P\)
satisfies \(2\partial_{\bar z}X+PX=0\). The product rule for
distributions gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-annulus-holomorphic-conjugation}
 2\partial_{\bar z}(X^{-1}GX)
 =X^{-1}(2\partial_{\bar z}G+PG-GP)X=0.
\end{equation}
The Weyl lemma for \(\bar\partial\), applied to
\eqref{eq:proof-annulus-holomorphic-conjugation}, makes \(X^{-1}GX\)
holomorphic.
On the exterior part of that disk it equals \(I\), so the identity
theorem gives \(X^{-1}GX=I\) everywhere on the disk. Hence \(G=I\)
on a neighborhood of every point of the circle. These neighborhoods
prove smoothness across it. Now \(G-I\) is a smooth solution of
the common relative equation on the connected annulus \(A\).
Lemma~\ref{lem:Gunique} propagates its vanishing throughout \(A\).
No global annular frame is required.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:complete-moment-relative-transport}
Let \(B\subset\mathbb C\) be a disk, with Euclidean area measure \(dA\),
and let \(A\) be a connected two-sided annulus of \(\partial B\).
Let \(P_1,P_2\) be smooth
\(m\times m\) matrix coefficients on a neighborhood of
\(\overline B\cup A\), with \(P_1=P_2\) on \(A\).
Suppose that smooth frames \(X_1,Y_2\) are invertible throughout
that neighborhood and satisfy
\[
 2\partial_{\bar z}X_1+P_1X_1=0,\qquad
 2\partial_{\bar z}Y_2-P_2^TY_2=0.
\]
Then the following conditions are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item For every holomorphic polynomial \(g\),
\[
 \int_B g(z)Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dA=0.
\]
\item There exists a smooth \(\mathrm{GL}(m,\mathbb C)\)-valued field
\(G\) on \(B\cup A\) satisfying
\[
 2\partial_{\bar z}G+P_1G-GP_2=0,\qquad G=I\quad\hbox{on }A.
\]
\end{enumerate}
When these conditions hold, \(G\) is unique. The moment identity
also holds for every \(g\in\mathcal O(B)\), without a boundary
regularity assumption on \(g\).
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Put \(\delta=2\partial_{\bar z}\) and
\(\mathsf W=Y_2^TX_1\). The frame equations give
\[
 \delta\mathsf W=-Y_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1.
\]
Assume \textup{(i)}. Cauchy--Green gives
\(\oint_{\partial B}z^n\mathsf W\,dz=0\) for every \(n\ge0\).
Consequently the exterior Cauchy transform is zero, by its expansion
at infinity and the identity theorem. The interior Cauchy factor
\[
 \mathsf R(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm i}\oint_{\partial B}
       \frac{\mathsf W(\zeta)}{\zeta-z}\,d\zeta
\]
is holomorphic and has boundary trace \(\mathsf W\), by the
Cauchy--Plemelj formula. The Fourier-decay argument of
Lemma~\ref{lem:matrix-plemelj} gives a smooth extension to
\(\overline B\).
Since \(\mathsf W\) is invertible throughout \(\overline B\), its
boundary determinant loop has winding number zero. The same boundary
loop is \(\det \mathsf R\), so the argument principle excludes zeros of
\(\det \mathsf R\) in \(B\). Thus \(\mathsf R\) and \(\mathsf R^{-1}\) are smooth
on the closed disk.

Both hypotheses used above are substantive: all polynomial
moments are needed to make the exterior Cauchy transform vanish,
whereas the invertibility of \(\mathsf W\) on the \emph{whole closed disk}
forces zero winding of \(\det\mathsf W\) on its boundary. Boundary
nonvanishing alone would not exclude interior zeros of \(\det \mathsf R\).

Set \(G=X_1\mathsf R^{-1}Y_2^T\) in \(B\). The product rule gives the
relative equation, and its boundary value is
\(X_1(Y_2^TX_1)^{-1}Y_2^T=I\).
Extend it by \(I\) on the exterior part of \(A\).
The equal traces cancel the contour contribution to its
distributional derivative. Because \(P_1=P_2\) on \(A\), the
extended field solves the relative equation there as a distribution.
On a small disk crossing the contour, choose, by Lemma~\ref{lem:matrix-dbar-frame}, an invertible local
frame \(X\) with \(\delta X=-P_1X\). Then
\(\delta(X^{-1}GX)=0\), and the Weyl lemma makes this matrix
holomorphic. It equals \(I\) on the exterior open part, so the
identity theorem gives \(G=I\) across the contour. Local
holomorphic uniqueness propagates that equality throughout the
connected annulus \(A\). The resulting field is smooth and
invertible on \(B\cup A\), proving \textup{(ii)}.

Conversely, assume \textup{(ii)} and define
\(\widetilde{\mathsf R}=Y_2^TG^{-1}X_1\).
Since
\(\delta G^{-1}=G^{-1}P_1-P_2G^{-1}\), the product rule in this
matrix order gives \(\delta\widetilde{\mathsf R}=0\).
The difference \(Q_*=\mathsf W-\widetilde{\mathsf R}\) vanishes on the
annulus and is therefore compactly supported inside \(B\).
For every \(g\in\mathcal O(B)\),
\[
 \int_BgY_2^T(P_1-P_2)X_1\,dA
 =-\int_B\delta(gQ_*)\,dA=0.
\]
Compact support justifies this integration without a boundary trace
of \(g\). This proves \textup{(i)} and the assertion for all
holomorphic weights.

For uniqueness, the difference of two relative fields solves the
homogeneous relative equation and vanishes on \(A\).
Local invertible frames for the two coefficients transform that
difference into a holomorphic matrix. The identity theorem on a
chain of overlapping disks then gives equality throughout the
connected set \(B\cup A\).
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:global-plemelj-gluing}
\label{lem:parameter-plemelj}
\label{rem:plemelj-global-convention}
Let \(p\) range in a smooth finite-dimensional real parameter chart.
In complex leaf coordinates suppose that the disks \(B_p\) and
two-sided boundary annuli are images of fixed reference sets under
\(\Phi_p(w)=a_pw+b_p\), where \(a_p\ne0\) and \(a_p,b_p\) are smooth.
Let \(P_{j,p}\) be jointly smooth connection matrices on neighborhoods
of these sets, with \(P_{1,p}=P_{2,p}\) throughout the annuli.
Assume that jointly smooth invertible frames extend across the closed disks
and annuli, satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-global-plemelj-frame-equations}
 2\partial_{\bar z}X_{1,p}+P_{1,p}X_{1,p}=0,\qquad
 2\partial_{\bar z}Y_{2,p}-P_{2,p}^TY_{2,p}=0,
\end{equation}
and have uniformly bounded derivatives and inverses on compact subcharts.
Set \(\mathsf W_p=Y_{2,p}^TX_{1,p}\), and suppose that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-global-plemelj-monomial-moments}
 \oint_{\partial B_p} z^n\mathsf W_p(z)\,dz=0
 \qquad(n\ge0).
\end{equation}
Then the Cauchy--Plemelj construction gives a unique invertible relative
transport \(G_p\), jointly smooth on the disks and annuli and equal to \(I\)
on the annuli, satisfying
\[
 2\partial_{\bar z}G_p+P_{1,p}G_p-G_pP_{2,p}=0.
\]
Different frames and enclosing disks give the same transport on each connected
overlap where their equations agree and both transports equal \(I\)
on a common nonempty open subset.

For bundle gluing, suppose additionally that the invariant relative
operators are covariant under smooth invertible bundle transition matrices
independent of \(x\), together with nonzero scalar changes of null
representative. Assume that each connected leaf component of a chart
overlap meets a common nonempty open set where both transports equal
\(I\). Then the transports obey the corresponding conjugation law
and define a global smooth bundle automorphism.

Suppose that this automorphism is defined on a common physical product
\(\mathcal U\times U\) in each spinor chart, with invariant equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-parameter-invariant-transport}
 2D_{\theta(\zeta)}G+\mathcal A_1(x,\zeta)G-G\mathcal A_2(x,\zeta)=0.
\end{equation}
Assume that the bundle transitions, the nonzero complex null representative
\(\theta\), and the matrices \(\mathcal A_j\) are holomorphic in \(\zeta\)
at fixed physical \(x\). Assume also that \(G=I\) on one fixed physical
open set \(E\subset\mathcal U\), and that each connected component of
\(\Pi\cap\mathcal U\), for every affine complex leaf \(\Pi\) and every
parameter, meets \(E\cap\Pi\) in a nonempty relatively open set.
Then \(G\) is holomorphic in the spinor parameter at fixed physical \(x\).
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Take the reference disk to be the unit disk and set
\(\mathsf W_p^\sharp=\mathsf W_p\circ\Phi_p\). The affine contour change gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-global-reference-moments}
 \oint_{|w|=1}w^n\mathsf W_p^\sharp(w)\,dw
 =a_p^{-1}\oint_{\partial B_p}
       \left(\frac{z-b_p}{a_p}\right)^n
       \mathsf W_p(z)\,dz=0,\qquad n\geq0.
\end{equation}
Since \(((z-b_p)/a_p)^n\) is a polynomial in \(z\), we use
\eqref{eq:proof-global-plemelj-monomial-moments} and linearity to prove
\eqref{eq:proof-global-reference-moments}. Applying
Lemma~\ref{lem:matrix-plemelj} to the jointly smooth invertible family
\(\mathsf W_p^\sharp\), we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Rplemelj}
 \mathsf R_p(w)=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathrm i}\oint_{|\zeta|=1}
             \frac{\mathsf W_p^\sharp(\zeta)}{\zeta-w}\,\,\mathrm d\zeta,
 \qquad |w|<1,
\end{equation}
with boundary trace \(\mathsf W_p^\sharp\). Its Fourier estimate
\eqref{eq:proof-plemelj-fourier-decay} and determinant argument give
joint smoothness of \(\mathsf R_p,\mathsf R_p^{-1}\) on the closed reference disks,
with all fixed finite derivative bounds on compact subcharts.

In physical leaf coordinates, we set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-plemelj-transport-product}
 F_p(z)=\mathsf R_p(\Phi_p^{-1}(z))^{-1},\qquad
 G_p(z)=X_{1,p}(z)F_p(z)Y_{2,p}(z)^T.
\end{equation}
The affine inverse is holomorphic in \(z\), so \(F_p\) is leafwise
holomorphic. Using \eqref{eq:proof-global-plemelj-frame-equations} and
\eqref{eq:proof-plemelj-transport-product}, we derive the relative
equation in \eqref{eq:Gtransport}. On the
contour \(F_p=\mathsf W_p^{-1}\), and
\(X_{1,p}(Y_{2,p}^TX_{1,p})^{-1}Y_{2,p}^T=I\). Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Gtransport}
 2\partial_{\bar z}G_p+P_{1,p}G_p-G_pP_{2,p}=0,\qquad
 G_p=I\quad\hbox{on }\partial B_p.
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:distributional-annulus-gluing} extends \(G_p\) by
\(I\) and gives \(G_p=I\) on the entire two-sided annulus. After
pullback this annulus is fixed locally in the parameters. The
jointly smooth closed-disk formula is therefore \(I\) on a fixed
inner collar and glues smoothly to \(I\) across the moving contour.
Invertibility inside follows from the three invertible factors in
\eqref{eq:proof-plemelj-transport-product}, and outside from \(G_p=I\).

Lemma~\ref{lem:Gunique} identifies two choices of frames or enclosing
disks on each connected overlap with the same equation and a common
normalization open set. On a bundle-chart overlap we conjugate one
transport by the prescribed transition matrix. Covariance makes it
solve the other chart's equation, after a nonzero scalar change of
null representative. Both transports equal \(I\) on an open part of
each connected leaf component, so the same uniqueness lemma proves
the conjugation law. The fundamental frames themselves need not obey
that transition.

For the final assertion, we set \(V=\partial_{\bar\zeta}G\).
Joint smoothness permits commuting this derivative with spatial
differentiation. Differentiating
\eqref{eq:proof-parameter-invariant-transport} and using holomorphicity
of \(\theta\) and \(\mathcal A_j\) gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-global-parameter-derivative}
 2D_{\theta(\zeta)}V+\mathcal A_1V-V\mathcal A_2=0.
\end{equation}

The derivative \(V=\partial_{\bar\zeta}G\) is taken in a
holomorphic quotient frame at fixed physical \(x\). The assumed
joint smoothness on \(\mathcal U\times U\) accounts for the
parameter dependence of the moving leaf coordinates. Since
\(G(x,\zeta)=I\) for every \(x\in E\) and every parameter,
\(V=0\) on that same fixed set. Only the reconstructed transport
is differentiated here.

At a fixed parameter, we choose \(b\ne0\) such that
\(b\theta=e_1+\mathrm i e_2\) is normalized. In its leaf coordinate
\(D_\theta=2b^{-1}\partial_{\bar z}\). Multiplication of
\eqref{eq:proof-global-parameter-derivative} by \(b/2\) puts the
equation for \(V\) in the form of Lemma~\ref{lem:Gunique}, with
coefficients \(b\mathcal A_j/2\). Each connected leaf component meets
\(E\) in a nonempty relatively open set, so that lemma gives \(V=0\)
on every component. Thus \(G\) is holomorphic at fixed physical \(x\).
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:spinor-overlap-plemelj}
Express the primal Plemelj transports \(G_0,G_\infty\) in the quotient
frames associated with the holomorphic spinors \((1,\zeta)\) and
\((\zeta^{-1},1)\), respectively. Let
\[
 D(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}\zeta I_2&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix}
\]
be the dual quotient transition matrix. On each connected leaf overlap
meeting their common exterior normalization open set, one has
\[
 G_0=D(\zeta)^TG_\infty D(\zeta)^{-T}.
\]
Thus these local transports glue as primal bundle automorphisms on
the overlaps used in the enclosing-ball construction.
<!-- end lemma-17 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
The spinor identities \eqref{eq:theta-spinor}--\eqref{eq:qspinor} and
the connection covariance \eqref{eq:connection-chart-scaling} give
\(\theta_0=\zeta^2\theta_\infty\) and
\(\mathcal A_{j,0}=\zeta^2D^T\mathcal A_{j,\infty}D^{-T}\).
Since \(D\) is independent of \(x\), the infinity-chart
relative equation is conjugated by \(D^T\) and then multiplied by
\(\zeta^2\) to give the equation for \(D^TG_\infty D^{-T}\) in the
zero chart. It is the same equation as for \(G_0\). Both transports
equal \(I\) on their common normalization open set.
Lemma~\ref{lem:Gunique} therefore proves the identity on the connected
leaf overlap. The primal coordinate change is \(D^T\); the matrix
\(D\) itself is the dual frame transition.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- lemma-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:representative-invariance}
Let \(b\in\mathbb C^*\) be independent of \(x\). The characteristic lines,
paired physical quotient bundles, and affine complex leaves of
\(\theta\) and \(b\theta\) are canonically identical. Under this
identification, a relative quotient transport solves the equation for
\(\theta\) if and only if it solves that for \(b\theta\). Its exterior
normalization and leafwise uniqueness are therefore independent of
the nonzero representative of the projective null direction.
<!-- end lemma-18 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Multiplication by \(b\) gives an invertible real rotation and dilation
on the pair \((\operatorname{Re}\theta,\operatorname{Im}\theta)\).
Their real span, and hence the affine leaf planes, are unchanged.
The symbol and derivative satisfy
\[
 A_{b\theta}=bA_\theta,\qquad D_{b\theta}=bD_\theta,
 \qquad \mathbb C e_{b\theta}=\mathbb C e_\theta.
\]
Use the same quotient basis under this canonical identification.
The induced relative operator is multiplied by \(b\), so its
solution set and normalization are unchanged. If coordinates are
instead supplied by rescaled spinors, the corresponding quotient
frame change must also be applied, as in
\eqref{eq:normalization-frame-change} below.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:frame-independence}
The relative transport is independent of the invertible primal and
dual \(\bar\partial\)-frames on a neighborhood of the closed leaf
disk used to form \(\mathsf W=Y_2^TX_1\). It is also independent of the
radius of a concentric enclosing disk whose boundary annulus lies
in the common exterior and which contains the entire support of
\(P_1-P_2\) on that leaf.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
For alternative frames, we define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-holomorphic-frame-changes}
 C_1=X_1^{-1}X_1',\qquad C_2=Y_2^{-1}Y_2'.
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:leaf-primal} and \eqref{eq:leaf-dual}, we prove that
the frame changes in \eqref{eq:proof-holomorphic-frame-changes} are
holomorphic. Their invertibility follows from that of the quotient frames.
On the contour the new product satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-alternative-plemelj-trace}
 \mathsf W'=C_2^T\mathsf W C_1=C_2^T\mathsf RC_1.
\end{equation}
Thus \(\mathsf R'=C_2^T\mathsf RC_1\) is its holomorphic Cauchy factor: the new
contour moments vanish by Cauchy's theorem, and the Cauchy formula
recovers this product. Consequently
\[
 X_1'(\mathsf R')^{-1}(Y_2')^T=X_1\mathsf R^{-1}Y_2^T=G.
\]
For two enclosing radii, we extend the smaller-disk transport by \(I\)
through the layer where \(P_1=P_2\). Lemma~\ref{lem:distributional-annulus-gluing}
makes this a smooth relative solution on the larger disk. It agrees
with the larger-disk transport on an exterior annulus, so the
uniqueness assertion of Theorem~\ref{thm:global-plemelj-gluing} identifies them.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:quotient-reconstruction}
For the common exterior extensions and fixed physical ball \(B_R\)
chosen in this section, assume \(\Lambda_1=\Lambda_2\). Then there is a unique
relative automorphism \(G\) of the rank-three primal quotient bundle
over \(B_R\times\mathbb{CP}^1\) which is jointly smooth in local
spatial and real chart coordinates, holomorphic in the projective
null direction at fixed \(x\), and equal to \(I\) on the fixed shell
\(E=\{R_0<|x|<R\}\). It intertwines the two quotient transports:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-reconstructed-primal-transport}
 2D_\theta G+\mathcal A_{1,\theta}G-G\mathcal A_{2,\theta}=0.
\end{equation}
Its local matrices obey \eqref{eq:G-chart-covariance}.
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
The construction from Theorem~\ref{thm:global-plemelj-gluing}, extended
using \eqref{eq:proof-physical-product-inside}--
\eqref{eq:proof-physical-product-outside}, verifies existence, invertibility,
and joint smoothness. Equation~\eqref{eq:G-chart-covariance} gives the
transition law, and \eqref{eq:proof-fixed-exterior-shell} specifies the fixed
exterior normalization. 
In each holomorphic spinor chart, \eqref{eq:theta-spinor} and
\eqref{eq:Gamma} give holomorphic direction and connection matrices.
The fixed product and the shell meeting every connected leaf section
were verified in \eqref{eq:proof-physical-product-inside}--
\eqref{eq:proof-fixed-exterior-shell}. The final assertion of
Theorem~\ref{thm:global-plemelj-gluing} therefore gives holomorphicity
of the relative transport at fixed \(x\).

For uniqueness, we subtract two such local matrices in the same quotient
frame. Their difference solves the homogeneous relative equation and
vanishes on \(E\). Each connected disk \(B_R\cap\Pi_{\theta,x_\perp}\)
meets that set in a relatively open subset, so Lemma~\ref{lem:Gunique}
gives equality on every section and hence on the whole ball.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:dual-relative}
Let \(G\) be the primal relative transport of Theorem~\ref{thm:quotient-reconstruction} and set
\[
 H=G^{-T}.
\]
Then \(H\) is smooth in \(x\), is a global holomorphic automorphism in the
projective null direction of the dual physical quotient bundle, equals the
identity on the fixed exterior shell \(E\), and satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dual-relative-transport}
 2D_\theta H+\Gamma_1H-H\Gamma_2=0,
\end{equation}
where \(\Gamma_j\) is the dual Lam\'e quotient connection \eqref{eq:Gamma}.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
In paired quotient coordinates, \(\Gamma_j=-\mathcal A_j^T\).
Differentiate the inverse transpose and use
\eqref{eq:proof-reconstructed-primal-transport} to obtain
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 2D_\theta H
 &=-H(2D_\theta G)^TH\\
 &=\mathcal A_1^TH-H\mathcal A_2^T
  =-\Gamma_1H+H\Gamma_2.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Rearranging gives \eqref{eq:dual-relative-transport}, with the order of the
two media unchanged. Inversion of an invertible smooth holomorphic
matrix preserves its spatial smoothness and parameter holomorphicity.
Also \(G=I\) on the fixed shell implies \(H=I\) there.
Taking inverse transposes in \eqref{eq:G-chart-covariance} gives
\eqref{eq:H-chart-covariance}. Thus the local matrices of \(H\)
define the dual bundle automorphism with all the asserted properties.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- lemma-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:Hform}
For each fixed \(x\), let \(H(x,\cdot):E^*\to E^*\) be a global
holomorphic automorphism. In a local spinor frame it has the form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hform}
 H=\begin{pmatrix}A(x)&0\\C(x;s,t)&\delta_H(x)\end{pmatrix},
\end{equation}
where \(A\in GL(2,\mathbb C)\), \(\delta_H\in\mathbb C^\times\), and \(C\) is a
\(1\times2\) row homogeneous of degree one in \((s,t)\).
If the local matrices of \(H\) are jointly smooth in \(x\) and the
real chart coordinates, then \(A\), \(\delta_H\), and the two
coefficient rows of \(C\) are smooth in \(x\).
<!-- end lemma-19 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Fix \(x\) and write the chart matrices in the \(2+1\) block
decomposition as
\[
 H_0(\zeta)=\begin{pmatrix}A_0(\zeta)&B_0(\zeta)\\
                          C_0(\zeta)&\delta_{H,0}(\zeta)\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 H_\infty(\upsilon)=\begin{pmatrix}A_\infty(\upsilon)&B_\infty(\upsilon)\\
                          C_\infty(\upsilon)&\delta_{H,\infty}(\upsilon)\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Every block is entire in its own affine chart coordinate. The dual
transition law \eqref{eq:H-chart-covariance}, written as
\[
 H_0(\zeta)=D(\zeta)^{-1}H_\infty(\zeta^{-1})D(\zeta)
\]
is equivalent on \(\zeta\ne0\) to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-h-block-transitions}
 \begin{aligned}
 A_0(\zeta)&=A_\infty(\zeta^{-1}),&
 \delta_{H,0}(\zeta)&=\delta_{H,\infty}(\zeta^{-1}),\\
 B_0(\zeta)&=\zeta^{-1}B_\infty(\zeta^{-1}),&
 C_0(\zeta)&=\zeta C_\infty(\zeta^{-1}).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:proof-h-block-transitions}, holomorphicity of the
infinity-chart blocks near \(\upsilon=0\)
makes \(A_0\) and \(\delta_{H,0}\) bounded for large \(|\zeta|\).
They are bounded on compact sets as well, so Liouville's theorem
applied entrywise makes them constant in \(\zeta\). We denote these
blocks by \(A(x)\) and \(\delta_H(x)\). The entire column \(B_0\)
is \(O(|\zeta|^{-1})\) at infinity, so the same theorem gives
\(B_0=0\), and \eqref{eq:proof-h-block-transitions} gives
\(B_\infty=0\).

Equation~\eqref{eq:proof-h-block-transitions} makes the entire row
\(C_0\) \(O(1+|\zeta|)\). Cauchy's estimates
on expanding circles show that every derivative of order at least
two vanishes. Hence there are \(1\times2\) rows \(C_s(x),C_t(x)\)
such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-h-affine-row}
 C_0(x,\zeta)=C_s(x)+\zeta C_t(x),\qquad
 C_\infty(x,\upsilon)=\upsilon C_s(x)+C_t(x).
\end{equation}
The expressions in \eqref{eq:proof-h-affine-row} are the restrictions
to \((1,\zeta)\) and \((\upsilon,1)\) of
the same homogeneous row
\[
 C(x;s,t)=sC_s(x)+tC_t(x).
\]
Thus the coefficient rows are common to both charts, even though
their coordinate expressions differ. Block triangularity gives
\(\det H=\det A\,\delta_H\); invertibility of \(H\) therefore forces
\(A\in GL(2,\mathbb C)\) and \(\delta_H\ne0\).

Finally, the coefficients can be recovered by fixed chart evaluations:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-h-coefficient-evaluations}
 \begin{gathered}
 A(x)=A_0(x,0),\qquad \delta_H(x)=\delta_{H,0}(x,0),\\
 C_s(x)=C_0(x,0),\qquad
 C_t(x)=C_0(x,1)-C_0(x,0).
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Joint smoothness of the chart matrices makes each function in
\eqref{eq:proof-h-coefficient-evaluations} smooth in \(x\). On compact spatial subsets, their derivatives of
every fixed finite order, \(A^{-1}\), and \(\delta_H^{-1}\) are bounded
by smoothness, nonvanishing, and compactness.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lemma-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:null-line-rigidity}
Let \(M_{\mathrm{null}}\in M_3(\mathbb C)\).  If
\[
 M_{\mathrm{null}}\theta\in\mathbb C\theta
 \qquad\text{for every }\theta\in\mathbb C^3\setminus\{0\}
 \text{ with }\theta\cdot\theta=0,
\]
then \(M_{\mathrm{null}}=qI_3\) for some \(q\in\mathbb C\).
<!-- end lemma-20 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Fix pairwise distinct indices \(i,j,k\). Apply the hypothesis to \(e_i\pm\mathrm i e_j\).
Their \(k\)-th coordinates vanish, so the \(k\)-th coordinates of their images give
\[
 (M_{\mathrm{null}})_{ki}+\mathrm i(M_{\mathrm{null}})_{kj}=0,\qquad (M_{\mathrm{null}})_{ki}-\mathrm i(M_{\mathrm{null}})_{kj}=0.
\]
Hence \((M_{\mathrm{null}})_{ki}=(M_{\mathrm{null}})_{kj}=0\). We use the triples
\((i,j,k)=(1,2,3),(1,3,2),(2,3,1)\) to eliminate every
off-diagonal entry. The matrix is now diagonal. For each \(i\ne j\),
the components with indices \(i\) and \(j\) of
\(M_{\mathrm{null}}(e_i+\mathrm i e_j)\in\mathbb C(e_i+\mathrm i e_j)\)
give \((M_{\mathrm{null}})_{ii}=(M_{\mathrm{null}})_{jj}\). All three diagonal entries therefore
coincide, so \(M_{\mathrm{null}}=qI_3\), with \(q=\operatorname{tr} M_{\mathrm{null}}/3\).
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lemma-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:tracefree-closure}
Let \(\mathcal D\subset\mathbb R^3\) be open, let
\(\rho\in C^3(\mathcal D)\), and let
\(\beta\in C^1(\mathcal D;\mathbb R^3)\). For a \(3\times3\) matrix
\(M_{\mathrm{test}}\), write \(\operatorname{tf} M_{\mathrm{test}}=M_{\mathrm{test}}-(\operatorname{tr} M_{\mathrm{test}})I_3/3\).
Define \(\mathscr H_\beta(x)v=\operatorname{tf}(\beta(x)\otimes v+v\otimes\beta(x))\).
Set \(r=\nabla\rho\), \(w=\Delta\rho\), and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-tensor}
 T_{ij}^{\ k}=\beta_i\delta_{jk}+\beta_j\delta_{ik}
                   -\frac23\delta_{ij}\beta_k.
\end{equation}
If
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-tracefree}
 \operatorname{tf}(\nabla^2\rho)=\operatorname{tf}(\beta\otimes r+r\otimes\beta)=\mathscr H_\beta(x)r,
\end{equation}
then \(U=(r_1,r_2,r_3,w)^T\) satisfies a closed first-order system
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-system}
 \partial_jU=\mathcal C_jU\qquad (j=1,2,3)
\end{equation}
with continuous \(4\times4\) coefficient matrices \(\mathcal C_j\).
Here all indices range from one to three, and repeated indices are
summed. More precisely,
\[
 \partial_jr_i=\frac13\delta_{ij}w+T_{ij}^{\ k}r_k,\qquad
 \partial_jw=A_jw+B_j^{\ k}r_k,
\]
where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-a-coefficient}
 A_j=\frac12\sum_iT_{ij}^{\ i}=\frac53\beta_j
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-b-coefficient}
 B_j^{\ k}=\frac32\left(\sum_i\partial_iT_{ij}^{\ k}
                  +\sum_{i,\ell}T_{ij}^{\ \ell}T_{\ell i}^{\ k}\right).
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-21 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Since \(\rho\in C^3\), its Hessian is symmetric, its third
derivatives commute, and \(U=(r,w)^T\) belongs to \(C^1\).
Restore the trace in \eqref{eq:proof-closure-tracefree}, using
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-tensor}, to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-restored-hessian}
 \partial_jr_i=\frac13\delta_{ij}w+T_{ij}^{\ k}r_k.
\end{equation}
Differentiate \eqref{eq:proof-closure-restored-hessian} in
\(x_i\) and sum over \(i\). The left side becomes
\(\partial_j\sum_i\partial_i r_i=\partial_jw\), while the trace
term contributes \(\partial_jw/3\). Substitute
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-restored-hessian} for \(\partial_i r_k\) to obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-laplacian-derivative}
 \frac23\partial_jw=(\partial_iT_{ij}^{\ k})r_k
       +T_{ij}^{\ k}\left(\frac13\delta_{ki}w+T_{ki}^{\ \ell}r_\ell\right).
\end{equation}
In \eqref{eq:proof-closure-laplacian-derivative}, we collect the
coefficient of \(w\) and rename the summed indices in the coefficient
of \(r\) to prove \eqref{eq:proof-closure-a-coefficient} and
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-b-coefficient}. By direct contraction of
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-tensor}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-linear-contractions}
 \sum_iT_{ij}^{\ i}=\frac{10}{3}\beta_j,\qquad
 \sum_i\partial_iT_{ij}^{\ k}
  =(\operatorname{div}\beta)\delta_{jk}+\partial_k\beta_j-\frac23\partial_j\beta_k.
\end{equation}
For the quadratic contraction of \eqref{eq:proof-closure-tensor},
symmetry of the two lower indices
and \(\sum_iT_{ii}^{\ k}=0\) give
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i,\ell}T_{ij}^{\ \ell}T_{\ell i}^{\ k}
 &=\sum_i\beta_iT_{ji}^{\ k}
   +\beta_j\sum_iT_{ii}^{\ k}
   -\frac23\sum_\ell\beta_\ell T_{\ell j}^{\ k}\\
 &=\frac13\left(|\beta|^2\delta_{jk}
                         +\frac13\beta_j\beta_k\right).
 \end{aligned}
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-closure-quadratic-contraction}
 \sum_{i,\ell}T_{ij}^{\ \ell}T_{\ell i}^{\ k}
 =\frac13|\beta|^2\delta_{jk}+\frac19\beta_j\beta_k.
\end{equation}
Substitute \eqref{eq:proof-closure-linear-contractions} and
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-quadratic-contraction} into
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-a-coefficient} and
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-b-coefficient} to obtain the closed-system matrices
\[
 \mathcal C_j=
 \begin{pmatrix}
   (T_{ij}^{\ k})_{i,k=1}^3 & (\delta_{ij}/3)_{i=1}^3\\
   (B_j^{\ k})_{k=1}^3 & 5\beta_j/3
 \end{pmatrix},
\]
where
\[
 B_j^{\ k}=\frac32\left[
  (\operatorname{div}\beta)\delta_{jk}+\partial_k\beta_j-\frac23\partial_j\beta_k
  +\frac13|\beta|^2\delta_{jk}+\frac19\beta_j\beta_k
 \right].
\]
The entries involve only \(\beta\), its first derivatives, and
their products, so \(\beta\in C^1\) makes them continuous.
The resulting system has no independent source term: every spatial
derivative of \(U\) is a linear combination of its four components.
The field \(U\) is already known to solve all three equations in
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-system};
propagation of its zero value along paths therefore requires no
flatness assumption on the matrices \(\mathcal C_j\).
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:rigidity}
Let \(\mathcal D\subset\mathbb R^3\) be connected and open, and let
\((\mu_j,\kappa_j)\in C^\infty(\mathcal D;\mathbb R^2)\), \(j=1,2\),
satisfy \(\mu_j>0\) and \(1/4<\kappa_j<1\). Assume that the
two pairs coincide on a nonempty exterior open set
\(E\subset\mathcal D\).

Suppose that their dual quotient connections \eqref{eq:Gamma} are
intertwined by a bundle automorphism \(H\) whose local matrices are
jointly smooth in \(x\) and the real spinor-chart coordinates,
holomorphic in the projective null direction for each fixed \(x\),
and satisfy the dual transition law \eqref{eq:H-chart-covariance}.
Assume also that \(H=I\) on \(E\) for every direction and that its
local matrices satisfy \eqref{eq:Hintertwine}. Then
\[
 \mu_1=\mu_2,\qquad \kappa_1=\kappa_2.
\]
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:Hform}, \(A\) and \(\delta_H\) are smooth and
direction-independent, and \(C=sC_s+tC_t\) has smooth coefficient rows.
The spinor representative is held fixed under spatial differentiation,
so \(D_\theta Q=D_\theta n=D_\theta\theta=0\).
Write
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \Gamma_j=\begin{pmatrix}M_j&N_j\\\mathsf P_j&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
 m_j=\nabla\log\mu_j,\qquad d_j=\kappa_j\sqrt{\mu_j},\\
 M_j=n\mathfrak r_{m_j},\qquad N_j=2d_jn,\qquad
 \mathsf P_j=-\theta^TF_{\mu_j}Q.
 \end{gathered}
\]
The row \(\mathsf P_j\) is the \(1\times2\) lower-left block of
\(\Gamma_j\); the leaf connection matrices \(P_j\) of Section~\ref{sec:transport}
have size \(3\times3\). Positivity gives \(d_j>0\)
everywhere, so division by \(d_j\) is legitimate.
Using \eqref{eq:Hform}, we split the intertwining equation
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Hintertwine}
 2D_\theta H+\Gamma_1H-H\Gamma_2=0
\end{equation}
into the four equations
\begin{align}
 2D_\theta A+M_1A+N_1C-AM_2&=0,\label{eq:H-block-ul}\\
 N_1\delta_H-AN_2&=0,\label{eq:H-block-ur}\\
 2D_\theta C+\mathsf P_1A-CM_2-\delta_H \mathsf P_2&=0,\label{eq:H-block-ll}\\
 2D_\theta\delta_H-CN_2&=0.\label{eq:H-block-lr}
\end{align}

Equations~\eqref{eq:H-block-ul}--\eqref{eq:H-block-lr} hold in
either affine spinor chart. They also
hold for every nonzero homogeneous spinor: by
\eqref{eq:theta-spinor} and \eqref{eq:qspinor}, under
\((s,t)\mapsto b(s,t)\), the quantities \(Q,n,C\) scale by
\(b\), \(\theta\) scales by \(b^2\), and \(A,\delta_H\) are
unchanged. The upper-left and lower-right equations have degree
two, the upper-right equation degree one, and the lower-left
equation degree three. This homogeneity carries the chart equations
to all representatives of the projective null cone.

The upper-right equation \eqref{eq:H-block-ur}, with the blocks
in \eqref{eq:Gamma}, gives
\[
 d_1\delta_H\,n=d_2An.
\]
At fixed \(x\), we apply \eqref{eq:H-block-ur} to the spinors
\((1,0)\) and \((0,1)\),
which give \(n=(0,1)^T\) and \(n=(-1,0)^T\), respectively.
The same scalar \(d_1\delta_H/d_2\) is therefore the multiplier
of \(A\) on both coordinate vectors of \(\mathbb C^2\). Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Aalpha}
 A=\alphaI_2,
 \qquad
 \alpha=\frac{d_1\delta_H}{d_2}.
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:Aalpha} and the blocks of \eqref{eq:Gamma}
into \eqref{eq:H-block-ul} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-alpha-equation}
 2(D_\theta\alpha)I_2+n\left[\alpha(\mathfrak r_{m_1}-\mathfrak r_{m_2})+2d_1C\right]=0.
\end{equation}
The outer product in \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-alpha-equation}
has rank at most one, whereas a nonzero scalar
multiple of \(I_2\) has rank two. Hence \(D_\theta\alpha=0\)
for every null direction. Since
\(2e_i=(e_i+\mathrm i e_j)+(e_i-\mathrm i e_j)\) for \(i\ne j\),
these null directions span \(\mathbb C^3\). Hence \(\nabla\alpha=0\),
and connectedness makes \(\alpha\) constant on \(\mathcal D\).
On \(E\), the equal coefficient pairs give
\(d_1=d_2\), and \(H=I\) gives \(\delta_H=1\). Thus \(\alpha=1\)
throughout \(\mathcal D\), and \eqref{eq:Aalpha} gives \(A=I_2\).
Set \(\alpha=1\) in \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-alpha-equation}
to obtain the remaining outer product in the form \(n\mathsf r=0\) with \(n\ne0\); its row
\(\mathsf r\) must vanish. Consequently
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Cblock}
 C=-\frac{\mathfrak r_{m_1-m_2}}{2d_1}.
\end{equation}
Set
\[
 \rho=\log(\mu_1/\mu_2).
\]
Positivity of the shear moduli makes \(\rho\) a smooth real function.
Equation~\eqref{eq:H-block-lr}, with \eqref{eq:Cblock}, and the
identity \(\mathfrak r_{\nabla\rho}n=D_\theta\rho\) from
\eqref{eq:Qn} and \eqref{eq:rmathfrak} give
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-delta-equation}
 2D_\theta\delta_H+\frac{d_2}{d_1}D_\theta\rho=0.
\end{equation}
Since \(\alpha=1\), \eqref{eq:Aalpha} gives
\(\delta_H=d_2/d_1>0\), so \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-delta-equation}
is \(2D_\theta\delta_H+\delta_H D_\theta\rho=0\).
Applying the integrating factor to \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-delta-equation}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-integrating-factor}
 D_\theta(\delta_H e^{\rho/2})
 =e^{\rho/2}\left(D_\theta\delta_H+\tfrac12\delta_H D_\theta\rho\right)=0
\end{equation}
for every null direction. The null vectors \(e_i\pm\mathrm i e_j\),
\(i\ne j\), span \(\mathbb C^3\), since
\(2e_i=(e_i+\mathrm i e_j)+(e_i-\mathrm i e_j)\).
Using \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-integrating-factor}, we therefore obtain
\(\nabla(\delta_H e^{\rho/2})=0\). Connectedness makes
\(\delta_H e^{\rho/2}\) constant on \(\mathcal D\). On \(E\),
\(\rho=0\) and \(\delta_H=1\), so the constant equals one. Hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-delta}
 \delta_H=e^{-\rho/2}.
\end{equation}
The definition
\eqref{eq:dmu} of \(d_j\) also gives
\[
 \frac{d_2}{d_1}=e^{-\rho/2}\frac{\kappa_2}{\kappa_1}.
\]
Combining \eqref{eq:dmu}, \eqref{eq:Aalpha}, and
\eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-delta}, with \(\alpha=1\), we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kappaequal}
 \kappa_1=\kappa_2=: \kappa.
\end{equation}

It remains to prove \(\rho=0\) from the lower-left equation
\eqref{eq:H-block-ll}; the other blocks have already given
\eqref{eq:kappaequal}. We write
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-gamma}
 \gamma=\frac{\nabla\rho}{\kappa\sqrt{\mu_1}}.
\end{equation}
With \(r=\nabla\rho\), \eqref{eq:rmathfrak} gives
\(\mathfrak r_{\nabla\rho}=-2r^TQ\), so \eqref{eq:Cblock} and
\eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-gamma} give
\(C=\gamma^TQ\). Equation~\eqref{eq:H-block-ll}, with \(A=I_2\), is
\[
 2D_\theta C-\theta^TF_{\mu_1}Q-Cn\mathfrak r_{m_2}
 +\delta_H\theta^TF_{\mu_2}Q=0.
\]
The identities \eqref{eq:Qn} and \eqref{eq:rmathfrak}, namely
\(Qn=-\theta/2\) and \(\mathfrak r_{m_2}=-2m_2^TQ\), give
\[
 Cn=-\frac{\gamma\cdot\theta}{2},\qquad
 -Cn\mathfrak r_{m_2}=-(\gamma\cdot\theta)m_2^TQ.
\]
With \((\nabla\gamma)_{ij}=\partial_j\gamma_i\), we also have
\(D_\theta C=\theta^T(\nabla\gamma)^TQ\).
Substituting \eqref{eq:Qn} and \eqref{eq:rmathfrak} into
\eqref{eq:H-block-ll} therefore gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-projected-equation}
 \theta^T\bigl[2(\nabla\gamma)^T-F_{\mu_1}
       +\delta_H F_{\mu_2}-\gamma m_2^T\bigr]Q=0.
\end{equation}
Both \(F_{\mu_j}\) are symmetric by \eqref{eq:Fmu}. We take the
bilinear transpose of \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-projected-equation},
using \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-delta}, to obtain
\(Q^T\mathcal K\theta=0\), where
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Kcal}
 \mathcal K=2\nabla\gamma-F_{\mu_1}+e^{-\rho/2}F_{\mu_2}-m_2\gamma^T.
\end{equation}
For every nonzero spinor, \eqref{eq:qspinor} gives
\(\theta^TQ=0\) and \(Q\) has rank two. For clarity,
\(Q(a,b)^T=0\) gives \(ta=0\), \(sb=0\), and \(sa-tb=0\), which imply
\(a=b=0\) even at \(s=0\) or \(t=0\). Thus \(\operatorname{im}Q=\theta^\perp\).
Nondegeneracy of the complex-bilinear dot product gives
\((\theta^\perp)^\perp=\mathbb C\theta\), including when \(\theta\) is null.
Equivalently, \(\ker Q^T=\mathbb C\theta\), so
\(\mathcal K\theta\in\mathbb C\theta\) for every null direction.
At each fixed \(x\), the matrix \(\mathcal K(x)\) of
\eqref{eq:Kcal} is independent of the spinor representative and appears in
the projected identities obtained from
\eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-projected-equation}: it has no direction dependence. Thus
Lemma~\ref{lem:null-line-rigidity} applies pointwise and gives
\(\mathcal K=q(x)I_3\), with the smooth scalar \(q=\operatorname{tr}\mathcal K/3\).

The scalar-matrix conclusion makes the symmetric trace-free part of
\eqref{eq:Kcal} vanish. To express that part in coefficient gradients
and the Hessian of \(\rho\), we put
\[
 r=\nabla\rho,
 \qquad
 k=\nabla\log\kappa,
 \qquad
 c_1=\mu_1^{-1/2}.
\]
By \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-delta},
\[
 \delta_H\mu_2^{-1/2}=\mu_1^{-1/2}=c_1.
\]
Moreover, \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-gamma} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-gamma-weight}
 \gamma=\frac{c_1}{\kappa}r,
 \qquad
 \nabla\log\left(\frac{c_1}{\kappa}\right)
 =-\frac12m_1-k.
\end{equation}
With the convention \((\nabla\gamma)_{ij}=\partial_j\gamma_i\),
differentiating \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-gamma-weight} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:grad-gamma-expanded}
 2\nabla\gamma
 =\frac{2c_1}{\kappa}\nabla^2\rho
 -\frac{c_1}{\kappa}r\otimes m_1
 -\frac{2c_1}{\kappa}r\otimes k.
\end{equation}
On the other hand, we use \eqref{eq:Fmu},
\eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-delta}, and \(m_1-m_2=r\) to obtain
\begin{align*}
 -F_{\mu_1}+\delta F_{\mu_2}
 =2c_1\Bigl[
 -\nabla^2\rho
 +(m_1\otimes m_1-m_2\otimes m_2)
 +\Xi_\rhoI
 \Bigr],
\end{align*}
where
\[
 \Xi_\rho=\Delta\rho-(m_1+m_2)\cdot r.
\]
The identity-matrix contribution from \eqref{eq:Fmu} disappears
under the trace-free projection. We use the identities
\[
 m_1\otimes m_1-m_2\otimes m_2
 =\frac12\Bigl[(m_1+m_2)\otimes r+r\otimes(m_1+m_2)\Bigr],
\]
\[
 -m_2\gamma^T=-\frac{c_1}{\kappa}m_2\otimes r,
\]
and the remaining product identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-mixed-product}
 r\otimes m_1+m_2\otimes r
 =\frac12\bigl[(m_1+m_2)\otimes r+r\otimes(m_1+m_2)\bigr]
\end{equation}
in \eqref{eq:Kcal}.
Combining \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-mixed-product}, \eqref{eq:Kcal},
\eqref{eq:grad-gamma-expanded}, and \eqref{eq:Fmu}, we obtain the full expansion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-rigidity-k-expansion}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\mathcal K}{c_1}={}&
 2\left(\frac1\kappa-1\right)\nabla^2\rho\\
 &+\left(1-\frac1{2\kappa}\right)
   \bigl[(m_1+m_2)\otimes r+r\otimes(m_1+m_2)\bigr]\\
 &-\frac2\kappa r\otimes k+2\Xi_\rho I_3.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Take the trace-free part of the symmetric part in
\eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-k-expansion},
\((\mathcal K+\mathcal K^T)/(2c_1)\), which vanishes because
Lemma~\ref{lem:null-line-rigidity} gives \(\mathcal K=qI_3\), and obtain
\begin{align}\label{eq:tf-hessian}
0={}&2\left(\frac1\kappa-1\right)\operatorname{tf}(\nabla^2\rho)\\
&+\operatorname{tf}\Bigg[
\left(1-\frac1{2\kappa}\right)
\left((m_1+m_2)\otimes r+r\otimes(m_1+m_2)\right)\notag\\
&\hspace{18mm}-\frac1\kappa\left(r\otimes k+k\otimes r\right)
\Bigg].\notag
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-k-expansion} also gives
\[
 \frac{\mathcal K-\mathcal K^T}{2}
 =-\frac{c_1}{\kappa}(r\otimes k-k\otimes r).
\]
Thus the skew-symmetric part of \eqref{eq:proof-rigidity-k-expansion},
with \(\mathcal K=qI_3\), additionally gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:skew-extra}
 r\otimes k-k\otimes r=0,
\end{equation}
although this identity is not needed below.

Since \eqref{eq:kappa-range} and \eqref{eq:kappaequal} give
\(1/4<\kappa<1\), the Hessian coefficient
\(2(1/\kappa-1)=2(1-\kappa)/\kappa\) is strictly positive.
Divide \eqref{eq:tf-hessian} by the Hessian coefficient and collect
the terms linear in \(r\) to obtain the smooth real vector field
\begin{equation}\label{eq:beta-explicit}
 \beta=\frac{2k-(2\kappa-1)(m_1+m_2)}{4(1-\kappa)}.
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{eq:tf-hessian} is precisely
\begin{equation}\label{eq:tf-linear}
 \operatorname{tf}(\nabla^2\rho)=\operatorname{tf}(\beta\otimes r+r\otimes\beta)
                    =\mathscr H_\beta(x)r,
\end{equation}
where, with indices ranging from 1 to 3 and repeated indices summed,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:T-explicit}
 T_{ij}^{\ k}=\beta_i\delta_{jk}+\beta_j\delta_{ik}
                   -\frac23\delta_{ij}\beta_k.
\end{equation}
Here \(T_{ij}^{\ k}=T_{ji}^{\ k}\) and \(\sum_iT_{ii}^{\ k}=0\).
For the fixed candidate pair, \(\beta\) and \(T\) are smooth coefficients
of a homogeneous linear system for \(U=(\nabla\rho,\Delta\rho)^T\).

Set \(w=\Delta\rho\) and \(U=(r_1,r_2,r_3,w)^T\).  Since
\(\mu_j,\kappa_j\in C^\infty\), we have \(\rho\in C^\infty\),
\(\beta\in C^\infty\), and hence \(T_{ij}^{\ k}\in C^1\).  Thus
Lemma~\ref{lem:tracefree-closure} applies to \eqref{eq:tf-linear} and
\eqref{eq:T-explicit}; hence \(U\) satisfies the closed system
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-system}, \(\partial_jU=\mathcal C_jU\),
with continuous \(4\times4\)
coefficient matrices. Since \(\rho=0\) on the open set \(E\), all
its derivatives vanish there; in particular \(r=0\), \(w=0\),
and hence \(U=0\) on \(E\).
Choose \(x_0\in E\) and an arbitrary \(x_1\in\mathcal D\). A piecewise
\(C^1\) path \(x(t)\subset\mathcal D\) joins them because
\(\mathcal D\) is connected and open. On each smooth segment, the chain rule and
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-system} give, for
\(V(t)=U(x(t))\),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-propagation-path-ode}
 V'(t)=\left(\sum_jx_j'(t)\mathcal C_j(x(t))\right)V(t).
\end{equation}
The coefficient in \eqref{eq:proof-propagation-path-ode} is continuous.
Using uniqueness for the linear ODE
\eqref{eq:proof-propagation-path-ode} with zero initial value, we obtain
\(V=0\) on successive segments.
Thus \(U(x_1)=0\), and \(U=0\) throughout \(\mathcal D\).
ODE uniqueness applies to the existing solution \(U\) of
\eqref{eq:proof-closure-system}; no flatness assumption on
the matrices \(\mathcal C_j\) is required.
Thus \(\nabla\rho=0\); connectedness and \(\rho|_E=0\)
give \(\rho=0\) on \(\mathcal D\).
The definition of \(\rho\) yields \(\mu_1=\mu_2\), and
\eqref{eq:kappaequal} gives \(\kappa_1=\kappa_2\). \qedhere
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
Lemmas~\ref{lem:common-extension} and \ref{lem:enlargement} give
smooth real strongly convex extensions to an origin-centered
enclosing ball \(B_R\), with equal DN maps and a common constant
exterior pair. We choose \(R_0<R\) with
\(\overline\Omega\subset B_{R_0}\), so the extensions coincide
outside \(\overline{B_{R_0}}\), and we set
\[
 E=\{x\in B_R:R_0<|x|<R\}.
\]
Fix the CGO balls and source ball as in Section~\ref{sec:transport}:
\[
 \overline{B_R}\Subset\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\Subset\Omega_1\Subset\Omega_0,
 \qquad \overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\subset\operatorname{int}K_{\mathrm{src}},
 \qquad K_{\mathrm{src}}\Subset\Omega_1,
\]
with both conjugated coefficient supports in \(\operatorname{int}K_{\mathrm{src}}\).

Theorem~\ref{thm:physical-nine-moments} supplies the fixed primal and
physical dual frames and their exact localized realizations. In the
original reduced variables these satisfy, columnwise,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-main-exact-fields}
 M_1\boldsymbol z_{1,h}=0,\qquad u_{1,h}=T_1\boldsymbol z_{1,h},\qquad
 y_{2,h}=S_2^Tu_{2,h},\qquad L_2u_{2,h}=0
 \quad\hbox{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}.
\end{equation}
The \(H^5\) reduced-remainder and \(H^3\) physical-adjoint bounds in
Theorem~\ref{thm:physical-nine-moments}, applied in
\eqref{eq:primal-adjoint}, give every entry of
\eqref{eq:leaf-moment} on each enclosing leaf disk. The limits are taken
at fixed direction and fixed tests, using the smooth frames selected
before the tests.

Applying Theorem~\ref{thm:quotient-reconstruction} to
\eqref{eq:leaf-moment} and the common exterior annuli, we obtain
a jointly smooth primal relative
automorphism \(G\) on \(B_R\times\mathbb{CP}^1\), holomorphic in the
projective direction at fixed \(x\), normalized on \(E\), and satisfying
\eqref{eq:proof-reconstructed-primal-transport} and
\eqref{eq:G-chart-covariance}.
Corollary~\ref{cor:dual-relative} gives \(H=G^{-T}\).
Using Lemma~\ref{lem:common-extension}, \eqref{eq:kappa-range},
Theorem~\ref{thm:quotient-reconstruction}, and
Corollary~\ref{cor:dual-relative}, we verify all hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:rigidity} on the connected domain
\(\mathcal D=B_R\), with the same \(E\). Applying
Theorem~\ref{thm:rigidity}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-main-coefficient-equality}
 \mu_1=\mu_2,\qquad \kappa_1=\kappa_2.
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:proof-main-coefficient-equality} in
\eqref{eq:lambda-kappa}, we obtain \(\lambda_1=\lambda_2\).
Restrict the extended coefficients to \(\Omega\) to complete the proof.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:appendix-physical-realization}
Fix a normalized complex null direction \(\theta\) and the nested
balls above. Let smooth amplitudes \(\widetilde a_0,\widetilde a_1\)
satisfy \eqref{eq:proof-app-transport-recursion} on the specified
neighborhoods, and suppose that
\(F=\chi_0\widetilde M\widetilde a_1\in H^k(\Omega_0)\)
for a fixed integer \(k\geq1\). Then there exists a correction
\(\widetilde r_h\), obtained from the \emph{same} right inverse at
Sobolev order \(k\), such that
\[
 \widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_h=e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}
    (\widetilde a_0+h\widetilde a_1+\widetilde r_h),
 \qquad \widetilde M\widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_h=0
 \quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},
\]
and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:appendix-cgo-two-sobolev}
 \|\widetilde r_h\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}\leq Ch^2\|F\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)},
 \qquad
 \|\widetilde r_h\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega_0)}\leq Ch\|F\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}.
\end{equation}
The constants are uniform in compact normalized-direction families
when the prescribed source norms are uniformly bounded. No support
condition is asserted for \(\widetilde r_h\) itself.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
Extend \(F\) by zero from \(\Omega_0\) and apply the
localized right inverse of Theorem~\ref{lem:ER-solvability}
at the selected order \(k\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-app-residual-correction}
 \widetilde r_h=-R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}(hF)
 =-R_{h,\theta}^{(k)}
                 (h\chi_0\widetilde M\widetilde a_1).
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse-equation}, the conjugated
reduced equation annihilates the leading residual on the region
where \(\chi_0=1\). The two bounds in
\eqref{eq:appendix-cgo-two-sobolev} follow from
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse}. In particular, for \(k=5\),
we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proof-app-cgo-estimate}
 \begin{gathered}
 \widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_h=e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}
       (\widetilde a_0+h\widetilde a_1+\widetilde r_h),\\
 \widetilde M\widetilde{\boldsymbol z}_h=0\quad\text{in }\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}},\\
 \|\widetilde r_h\|_{H^5(\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}})}
 \leq C h^2\|\chi_0\widetilde M\widetilde a_1\|_{H^5(\Omega_0)}.
 \end{gathered}
\end{equation}


Applying the same localized right inverse, with $k=5$ in
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse}, to the source
$h\chi_0\widetilde M\widetilde a_1$ yields both ordinary-Sobolev bounds

\begin{equation}\label{eq:app-H5-H6-remainder}
\begin{aligned}
 \|\widetilde r_h\|_{H^5(\Omega_0)}
       &\leq Ch^2\|\chi_0\widetilde M\widetilde a_1\|_{H^5(\Omega_0)},\\
 \|\widetilde r_h\|_{H^6(\Omega_0)}
       &\leq Ch\|\chi_0\widetilde M\widetilde a_1\|_{H^5(\Omega_0)}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

For a prescribed first correction, an \(H^7\) bound on the cutoff
support controls the source norm. For a constructed correction, the
fixed cutoffs and the \(C_{x_\perp}^9C_z^{8,\alpha}\) coefficient, amplitude,
frame, inverse, and partition-weight bounds in
Theorem~\ref{thm:autonomous-cgo} suffice. Multiplication by the common
\(\mathcal R\) preserves Sobolev orders. The reduced \(H^5\) error is
therefore \(O(h^2)\) on the entire working ball, and the additional
\(h^{-1}J_\theta r_h\) term is \(O(h)\) in \(H^3\), retaining
\(K_0a_0-J_\theta a_1\).
<!-- end proof-37 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{rem:appendix-minimal-moment-regularity}
For a supplementary physical adjoint polarization with
\(J_\theta a_0=0\), the normalized lift has an error consisting of
\(hK_0a_1-h^{-1}J_\theta r_h+K_0r_h\).
Since \(J_\theta\) has order at most one and \(K_0\) order at most
two, the estimates from Proposition~\ref{prop:appendix-physical-realization}
at \(k=1\) imply, for fixed smooth amplitudes,
\[
 \|hK_0a_1-h^{-1}J_\theta r_h+K_0r_h\|_{L^2}
 \leq C\bigl(h\|a_1\|_{H^2}
       +h^{-1}\|r_h\|_{H^1}+\|r_h\|_{H^2}\bigr)
 =O(h).
\]
This is enough for the \(L^2\)-based Betti moment limit.
The \(k=5\) specialization is retained for the stronger
\(H^3\) physical lift estimate and the pointwise rank control
of Theorem~\ref{thm:graded-frame}. The exact correction is not
assumed to vanish on the region where the source cutoff is one.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- lemma-22: body_md -->
\label{lem:appendix-free-inverse}
Let \(P_\delta^0=(D+\delta)^2\), with
\(\delta=\tau(\omega_0+i\nu)\), where \(\tau\geq1\) and
\(\omega_0,\nu\) form a real orthonormal pair. The Fourier multiplier
\(E_\delta\) with symbol \(1/p_\delta\), interpreted by its locally
integrable representative, satisfies
\(P_\delta^0 E_\delta f=f\) and, for every integer \(j\geq0\),
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-free-estimates}
 \|E_\delta f\|_{H^j_{-1}}\leq C_j\tau^{-1}\|f\|_{H^j_1},
 \qquad
 \|E_\delta f\|_{H^{j+1}_{-1}}\leq C_j\|f\|_{H^j_1}.
\end{equation}
The constants are uniform under rotations of the orthonormal pair.
<!-- end lemma-22 -->
<!-- proof-38: body_md -->
The estimates \eqref{audrev-free-estimates} follow from a one-dimensional
factorization of the free operator.  Rotate so that \(\omega_0=e_1,\nu=e_2\), write \(t=x_2\), and
set \(u=e^{-\mathrm i\tau x_1}v\).
After Fourier transformation in \((x_1,x_3)\), the free operator on
\(v\) is
\[
 L_b=-\partial_t^2+2\tau\partial_t+b^2-\tau^2
     =-(\partial_t-a_+)(\partial_t-a_-),
 \quad b=(\eta_1^2+\eta_3^2)^{1/2},\quad a_\pm=\tau\pm b.
\]
For a nonzero real \(a\), let
\[
 G_a(t)=
 \begin{cases}
  -{\bf1}_{(-\infty,0)}(t)e^{at},&a>0,\\
  {\bf1}_{(0,\infty)}(t)e^{at},&a<0.
 \end{cases}
\]
Then \((\partial_t-a)G_a=\delta_0\).
The convolution kernel \(K_b=-G_{a_+}*G_{a_-}\) of \(L_b^{-1}\)
satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-green-bounds}
 |K_b|\leq(\tau+b)^{-1},\qquad
 b|K_b|\leq1,\qquad |K_b'|\leq2.
\end{equation}
Indeed \(\|G_{a_+}\|_{L^1}=(\tau+b)^{-1}\),
\(\|G_{a_-}\|_{L^\infty}\leq1\), and
\(K_b'=a_-K_b-G_{a_+}\).
At \(b=\tau\), one may take \(G_0={\bf1}_{(0,\infty)}\);
the set of tangential frequencies $\{b=\tau\}$ has measure zero.
For $\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}>1/2$ put $w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(t)=\langle t\rangle^{-\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}$
and $C_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}=\int_{\mathbb R}w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(t)^2\,dt$. The pointwise kernel
bounds \eqref{audrev-green-bounds} yield the following weighted
Hilbert--Schmidt estimates (for almost every tangential frequency $b$):

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-green-HS-estimates}
\begin{aligned}
 \|w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(t)K_b(t-s)w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(s)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2_{t,s})}
    &\leq C_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(\tau+b)^{-1},\\
 \|w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(t)bK_b(t-s)w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(s)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2_{t,s})}
    &\leq C_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}},\\
 \|w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(t)K_b'(t-s)w_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}(s)\|_{L^2(\mathbb R^2_{t,s})}
    &\leq 2C_{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

Partial Plancherel, followed by
\(\langle x\rangle^{-\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}\leq\langle t\rangle^{-\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}\) for the resulting function and \(\langle t\rangle^{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}\leq\langle x\rangle^{\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}}\)
for the source function, proves the weighted \(L^2\) estimate and its first
derivative version.  The modulation in \(x_1\) costs the additional
term \(\tau K_b\), which is bounded by one.

The direct integral of the kernels \(K_b\) gives the Fourier multiplier
\(1/p_\delta\), with no additional distribution on its characteristic set.
For \(b\ne\tau\), its Fourier transform in \(t\) is
\[
 -\big[(\mathrm i\eta_2-a_+)(\mathrm i\eta_2-a_-)\big]^{-1}
  =\big[\eta_2^2+2\mathrm i\tau\eta_2+b^2-\tau^2\big]^{-1}.
\]
Near \(b=\tau,\eta_2=0\), the real and imaginary parts on the
right have a nonsingular two-dimensional Jacobian.  Its reciprocal
is thus locally integrable.  Fubini on compact frequency sets and
then on Schwartz test functions identifies the direct-integral
kernel with this locally integrable reciprocal.  The value selected
at \(b=\tau\) does not change the direct integral.
Undoing the modulation gives \(1/p_\delta\) and
\(p_\delta E_\delta=I\).
The free-inverse estimates \eqref{audrev-free-estimates} extend from Schwartz functions by density.
Since the original \(E_\delta\) commutes with constant-coefficient
derivatives, the \(L^2\) and first derivative estimates with
\(\rho_{\mathrm{wt}}=1\) imply \eqref{audrev-free-estimates} for every integer \(j\).
<!-- end proof-38 -->
<!-- lemma-23: body_md -->
\label{lem:appendix-local-error}
Fix the nested domains, compact coefficient supports, spatial cutoffs,
and a compact family of normalized directions as above. For every
fixed integer \(j\geq0\), the operator \(Q_\tau\) constructed from
the paired symbols in \eqref{audrev-parametrix} satisfies
\[
 P_\delta Q_\tau=\psi_3I_m+S_\tau+\mathcal E_\tau,
 \qquad
 \|\psi_3(S_\tau+\mathcal E_\tau)\|_{H^j\to H^j}
 \leq C_j\tau^{-1/2},
\]
and
\[
 \|\psi_2Q_\tau g\|_{H^j}\leq C_j\tau^{-1}\|g\|_{H^j},\qquad
 \|\psi_2Q_\tau g\|_{H^{j+1}}\leq C_j\|g\|_{H^j}.
\]
All bounds are uniform on the indicated compact family of directions.
The smallness assertion concerns the \emph{localized} error; no
smallness of \(P_\delta Q_\tau-I\) on the whole space is claimed.
<!-- end lemma-23 -->
<!-- proof-39: body_md -->
Use left, or Kohn--Nirenberg, quantization
\[
 \operatorname{Op}(a)u(x)
  =(2\pi)^{-3}\int e^{\mathrm ix\cdot\xi}a(x,\xi)\widehat u(\xi)\,d\xi.
\]
The operators and their composition remainders are estimated on
\(H^j\) using their full Fourier kernels, including spatial tails.
For a symbol with fixed compact \(x\)-support and bounded
derivatives in \(x\), its partial Fourier transform satisfies
\[
 |\widehat a(\eta-\xi,\xi)|\leq C_N\langle\eta-\xi\rangle^{-N}.
\]
Schur's test with
\(\langle\eta\rangle^j/\langle\xi\rangle^j
 \leq C_j\langle\eta-\xi\rangle^j\)
therefore proves boundedness on \(H^j\).
More precisely, for compactly supported $x$-symbols with
uniform bounds on the spatial derivatives through sufficiently high order,

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-unweighted-pdo-bound}
 \|\operatorname{Op}(a)u\|_{H^j}
 \leq C_j\|u\|_{H^j}.
\end{equation}

Here $C_j$ depends on the fixed spatial support and finitely
many uniform symbol seminorms.
If a compactly supported symbol \(c\) is composed on the right
with such an \(a\), the difference from the product symbol has
Fourier kernel
\[
 \int
  \big[\widehat a(\eta-\zeta,\zeta)
       -\widehat a(\eta-\zeta,\xi)\big]
       \widehat c(\zeta-\xi,\xi)\,d\zeta.
\]
If the compact-support symbol seminorms of $\partial_\xi a$ used in \eqref{audrev-unweighted-pdo-bound} are
\(O(\varepsilon)\), the mean value theorem bounds this kernel by
\[
 C_N\varepsilon\int
 |\zeta-\xi|\langle\eta-\zeta\rangle^{-N}
              \langle\zeta-\xi\rangle^{-N}\,d\zeta.
\]
Choosing \(N\) large and applying Schur again gives an
\(O(\varepsilon)\) norm on \(H^j\).
Under the stated uniform symbol assumptions on $a,c$ and
the $O(\varepsilon)$ compact-support seminorm bounds for $\partial_\xi a$,
this reads

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-unweighted-composition-bound}
 \|\operatorname{Op}(a)\operatorname{Op}(c)
       -\operatorname{Op}(ac)\|_{H^j\to H^j}
 \leq C_j\varepsilon.
\end{equation}

When \(a\) is a Fourier multiplier the kernel is instead
\([a(\eta)-a(\xi)]\widehat c(\eta-\xi,\xi)\), with the same bound.
The composition estimate applies to
\(\operatorname{Op}(b_a)\psi_3\operatorname{Op}(d_a)\), since its right
symbol \(\psi_3d_a\) has compact \(x\)-support. The Fourier-kernel
bounds include the operators' spatial tails.

An additional estimate is needed from a weighted Sobolev space for a symbol with compact spatial support.
For \(a\) with fixed compact \(x\)-support and bounded \(x\)
derivatives of \(a,\partial_\xi a,\partial_\xi^2a\), the exact
identity on Schwartz functions is
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-weighted-pdo-identity}
 \operatorname{Op}(a)\langle x\rangle^2
 =\langle x\rangle^2\operatorname{Op}(a)
   -2\mathrm i\sum_j x_j\operatorname{Op}(\partial_{\xi_j}a)
   -\operatorname{Op}(\Delta_\xi a).
\end{equation}
The unweighted \(H^j\) bound from Schur's test, applied to each
symbol with compact spatial support in \eqref{audrev-weighted-pdo-identity}, proves
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-weighted-pdo}
 \|\operatorname{Op}(a)u\|_{H^j}
       \leq C_j\|u\|_{H^j_{-1}}.
\end{equation}
Here set \(v=\langle x\rangle^{-2}u\), for which
\(\|v\|_{H^j}\leq C_j\|u\|_{H^j_{-1}}\), and apply
\eqref{audrev-weighted-pdo-identity} to \(v\).
Smooth compactly supported functions are dense in the weighted Sobolev space defined in \eqref{audrev-weighted-Sobolev-norm}; the estimate defines the unique
bounded extension and preserves the distributional identity.
Thus two frequency derivatives, together with finitely many
spatial derivatives, suffice for the weighted passage.
This argument does not discard a nonlocal tail.

Taking \(\varepsilon=\tau^{-1/2}\) in the composition estimate and
using \eqref{audrev-symbol-product} gives
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-composition-error}
 S_\tau=\sum_{a\geq0}\operatorname{Op}(b_a)\,
              \psi_3\operatorname{Op}(d_a)-\psi_3 I_m,
 \qquad \|S_\tau\|_{H^j\to H^j}\leq C_j\tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
The subtracted multiplication operator is \(\psi_3 I_m\).
In particular no identity assertion is made in the transition
region of the source cutoff.


Define
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-parametrix}
 Q_\tau g=\sum_{a\geq0}
       \operatorname{Op}(b_a)E_\delta
                 \psi_3\operatorname{Op}(d_a)g.
\end{equation}
For Schwartz functions the exact differential calculation is

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-pdo-differential-error-decomposition}
\begin{aligned}
 P_\delta\operatorname{Op}(b_a)E_\delta
   &=\operatorname{Op}(b_a)
        +\operatorname{Op}(r_{0,a})E_\delta
        +\operatorname{Op}(r_{1,a})E_\delta,\\
 r_{0,a}&=-\Delta_x b_a-2\mathrm i A\cdot\nabla_xb_a+q b_a,\\
 r_{1,a}&=2(\xi+\delta)\cdot(A b_a-\mathrm i\nabla_xb_a).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

All identities extend to the functions in the Sobolev spaces used below by the
bounds that follow.
Only the error localized by \(\psi_3\) is needed.  In this region
\(\chi_{\mathrm{sp}}=1\), and \(\psi_3r_{0,a}\) has bounded seminorms and compact spatial support.  Equations
\eqref{audrev-free-estimates} and \eqref{audrev-weighted-pdo}
give an \(O(\tau^{-1})\) bound on \(H^j\) for its contribution to
\(\psi_3P_\delta Q_\tau\).

For $a\geq1$, the symbol cancellation
\eqref{audrev-symbol-transport-cancellation}, derived from
\eqref{audrev-frame-transport}, removes the component
$\tau(\omega+\mathrm i\nu)$ from the first-order error. The remaining vector is
\[
 \xi+\delta-\tau(\omega+\mathrm i\nu)
       =(r-\tau)\omega+\xi_\nu\nu,
\]
whose length is \(O(\sqrt\tau)\) on the support of the near
cutoff.  More precisely,

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-near-error-symbol-bound}
 |\partial_x^\alpha\partial_\xi^\beta(\psi_3r_{1,a})|
       \leq C_{\alpha\beta}\tau^{(1-|\beta|)/2}.
\end{equation}

By \eqref{audrev-near-error-symbol-bound},
\(\tau^{-1/2}\psi_3r_{1,a}\) has the seminorms needed in
\eqref{audrev-weighted-pdo}.
Apply \(E_\delta\) first to the compactly supported source
\(\psi_3\operatorname{Op}(d_a)g\), use its \(H^j_1\)-norm and
\eqref{audrev-free-estimates}, and then apply \(\operatorname{Op}(\psi_3r_{1,a})\).
The resulting contribution is \(O(\tau^{-1/2})\) on \(H^j\).
No derivative of \(1/p_\delta\) has been taken near its
characteristic set.

For \(a=0\), the first-order error is
\(2A\cdot(\xi+\delta)\chi_F\).
On the support of $\chi_F$, the free symbol satisfies

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-far-symbol-ellipticity}
 |p_\delta(\xi)|\geq
 \begin{cases}
 c\tau^{3/2},&|\xi|\leq4\tau,\\
 c|\xi|^2,&|\xi|\geq4\tau.
 \end{cases}
\end{equation}

Indeed either \(|\xi_\nu|\geq\sqrt\tau\), giving the first bound from
the imaginary part, or
\(|r^2-\tau^2|\geq\tau^{3/2}\) and \(\xi_\nu^2\leq\tau\),
giving it from the real part for large \(\tau\).
The second bound follows from $|\xi|\geq4\tau$ and
$|p_\delta(\xi)-|\xi|^2|\leq 2|\delta||\xi|$.
The ratio
\[
 a_F(x,\xi)=\frac{2A(x)\cdot(\xi+\delta)\chi_F(\xi)}
                         {p_\delta(\xi)}
\]
is defined by zero near the characteristic set.  In the first
region, differentiating the reciprocal costs at most
\(\tau^{-1/2}\) per derivative; in the second it gives the usual
\(|\xi|^{-1}\) decay per derivative.  Consequently

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-far-error-symbol-bound}
 |\partial_x^\alpha\partial_\xi^\beta a_F|
        \leq C_{\alpha\beta}\tau^{-1/2-|\beta|/2}.
\end{equation}

The unweighted estimate for symbols with compact spatial support
in \eqref{audrev-unweighted-pdo-bound}, applied to the symbol
estimate \eqref{audrev-far-error-symbol-bound}, shows that
$\operatorname{Op}(a_F)$ has operator norm $O(\tau^{-1/2})$ on $H^j$.
Here the identity between the differential-error operator and
\(\operatorname{Op}(a_F)\) is legitimate because \(E_\delta\)
is a Fourier multiplier and the numerator vanishes on a
neighborhood of its characteristic set.
Only the far-frequency symbol $a_F$, bounded in \eqref{audrev-far-error-symbol-bound}, requires derivatives of $1/p_\delta$.

It follows that
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-local-error}
 P_\delta Q_\tau=\psi_3 I_m+S_\tau+\mathcal E_\tau,\qquad
 \|\psi_3\mathcal E_\tau\|_{H^j\to H^j}
           \leq C_j\tau^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
The equality is distributional; the estimate concerns the
localized error displayed, not an error at the remote spatial
cutoff.
For the solution itself, apply \eqref{audrev-weighted-pdo} to
\(\psi_2b_a\), including the multiplier term \(a=0\).
The compactly supported sources
\(\psi_3\operatorname{Op}(d_a)g\) have
\(H^j_1\)-norm bounded by \(C_j\|g\|_{H^j}\).
The two bounds in \eqref{audrev-free-estimates} therefore yield
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-Q-estimates}
 \|\psi_2Q_\tau g\|_{H^j}\leq C_j\tau^{-1}\|g\|_{H^j},
 \qquad
 \|\psi_2Q_\tau g\|_{H^{j+1}}\leq C_j\|g\|_{H^j}.
\end{equation}
