In the second estimate the first operator is bounded from
\(H^{j+1}_{-1}\) to \(H^{j+1}\); no \(H^{j+1}\) norm of the
source or of \(g\) is required.
<!-- end proof-39 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:appendix-interior-right-inverse}
Let \(m\geq1\), \(A_j,q\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^3;M_m(\mathbb C))\),
and fix concentric balls
\(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\Subset\Omega_1\Subset\Omega_0\)
and a closed source ball \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\Subset\Omega_1\)
whose interior contains the coefficient supports and
\(\overline\Omega_{\mathrm{CGO}}\). Consider \(P_\delta\) from
\eqref{audrev-system-operator} with
\(\delta=\tau(\omega_0+i\nu)\) and a compact family of real
orthonormal pairs \((\omega_0,\nu)\). For each fixed integer
\(k\geq0\), all sufficiently large \(\tau\), and every
\(f\in H^k(\Omega_0;\mathbb C^m)\) supported in \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\),
there is a \emph{single linear} operator \(\mathcal R_\delta\) with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:appendix-two-order-interior}
 P_\delta\mathcal R_\delta f=f\quad\text{in }\Omega_1,\qquad
 \tau\|\mathcal R_\delta f\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}
 +\|\mathcal R_\delta f\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega_0)}
 \leq C_k\|f\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}.
\end{equation}
The constants are uniform for a compact set of normalized null
directions. No boundary condition on \(\partial\Omega_0\) is imposed.
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-40: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{lem:appendix-local-error}, for the prescribed
fixed integer \(k\), set
\[
 T_\tau=\psi_3(S_\tau+\mathcal E_\tau):H^k\longrightarrow H^k.
\]
Its values have compact support, and its operator norm is at most
\(C_k\tau^{-1/2}\).
For large \(\tau\), set
\[
 g=(I+T_\tau)^{-1}f,\qquad
 \mathcal R_\delta f=\psi_2Q_\tau g.
\]
A source supported in \(K_{\mathrm{src}}\Subset\Omega_1\) is extended by zero
from \(\Omega_0\); this is a bounded operation at every integer
Sobolev order under consideration, since it vanishes in a
neighborhood of the boundary.  On \(\Omega_1\) both \(\psi_2\)
and \(\psi_3\) equal one.  Equation
\eqref{audrev-local-error} and the equation for \(g\) give

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-final-interior-right-inverse}
\begin{gathered}
 P_\delta\mathcal R_\delta f=f\quad\hbox{in }\Omega_1,\\
 \tau\|\mathcal R_\delta f\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}
       +\|\mathcal R_\delta f\|_{H^{k+1}(\Omega_0)}
          \leq C_k\|f\|_{H^k(\Omega_0)}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}

Both bounds apply to \(\mathcal R_\delta\), constructed with the single
Neumann inverse \((I+T_\tau)^{-1}\) on \(H^k\).
The commutator with the final spatial cutoff vanishes on
\(\Omega_1\); it need not be small on the transition region.
The operator $\mathcal R_\delta$ is a localized right inverse
by \eqref{audrev-final-interior-right-inverse}; no boundary
condition is imposed on \(\partial\Omega_0\).
The finite parameter cover used above gives uniform constants
for a compact family of directions.  It does not assert
parameter derivatives of the exact right inverse.

Finally take
\[
 A_j=\frac{\widetilde A_j}{2\mathrm i},\qquad
 q=-\widetilde B,\qquad
 \delta=\mathrm i\theta/h,\qquad \tau=h^{-1}.
\]
Then \(P_\delta=-e^{\theta\cdot x/h}\widetilde M
e^{-\theta\cdot x/h}\).
Applying the localized right-inverse identity and the two
unweighted Sobolev estimates \eqref{audrev-final-interior-right-inverse}
to the source $-f$, with $\tau=h^{-1}$, gives
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse-equation} and
\eqref{eq:system-cgo-right-inverse}.
For \(k=5\) this is an ordinary \(H^5\) estimate and an
ordinary \(H^6\) estimate, rather than a semiclassical estimate.
<!-- end proof-40 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:appendix-boundary-jets}
Let \(\Omega\subset\mathbb R^3\) be a bounded smooth domain. Suppose that
\((\lambda_j,\mu_j)\in C^\infty(\overline\Omega;\mathbb R^2)\),
\(j=1,2\), satisfy the strong convexity assumptions
\eqref{eq:convexity}, and that their full elastic Dirichlet-to-Neumann
maps, with the outward traction convention \eqref{eq:DN}, coincide.
Then in every inward boundary-normal chart \((y,r_{\mathrm{in}})\)
and for every fixed integer \(j\geq0\),
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-boundary-all-jets}
 \partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\lambda_1(y,0)
   =\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\lambda_2(y,0),\qquad
 \partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\mu_1(y,0)
   =\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\mu_2(y,0).
\end{equation}
These identities hold on every boundary component, and tangential
differentiation gives equality of all mixed boundary derivatives.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- lemma-24: body_md -->
\label{lem:appendix-stable-graph}
At a boundary point, freeze the strongly convex Lam\'e coefficients
and fix a nonzero tangential covector of length \(k\). With the
matrices \(\mathsf Z_0\) and \(N\) in
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-principal}--\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-jordan},
the three-dimensional decaying displacement space is represented by
\[
 U_f(r_{\mathrm{in}})=e^{-kr_{\mathrm{in}}}
       (I_3-kr_{\mathrm{in}}N)f,\qquad f\in\mathbb C^3.
\]
Its Dirichlet boundary projection is an isomorphism. In particular,
the frozen Dirichlet problem satisfies the complementing condition,
even though its stable characteristic root is repeated.
<!-- end lemma-24 -->
<!-- proof-41: body_md -->
The flux system \eqref{audrev-eq-layered-flux} and the algebraic
identity in \eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-principal} give the stable
normal evolution generator \(-kK_{\mathrm{st}}=-k(I_3+N)\).
Since \(N^2=0\), its exponential is exactly the displayed
\(U_f\). It decays for every \(f\), while \(U_f(0)=f\).
The frozen normal characteristic polynomial is
\(\mu^2(\lambda+2\mu)(\rho_{\mathrm{char}}^2+k^2)^3\);
its stable space has complex dimension three, counting the
Jordan structure. Hence these solutions exhaust that space, and
the Dirichlet trace on it is bijective. Rotational invariance
establishes the assertion for every nonzero tangential covector.
<!-- end proof-41 -->
<!-- lemma-25: body_md -->
\label{lem:appendix-jet-invertibility}
For the layered model above, the coefficient of order \(k^{1-j}\)
in the asymptotic impedance symbol depends on normal derivatives
of the Lam\'e parameters of order at most \(j\). For each fixed
\(j\geq1\), after subtracting the contributions of lower-order
normal derivatives, its new \(j\)-th derivative part is given by
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-highest} and the two scalar observations
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-two-observations}. The latter determine
\(\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\mu\) and
\(\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\lambda\) uniquely; their determinant is
\[
 2^{1-2j}\frac{\mu^2}{(\lambda+3\mu)^2}>0.
\]
This includes all strongly convex coefficients, without any
restriction on the sign of \(\lambda\) beyond strong convexity.
<!-- end lemma-25 -->
<!-- proof-42: body_md -->
Expand the layered symbol as
\[
 \mathfrak Z(r_{\mathrm{in}},k)\sim k\mathsf Z_0(r_{\mathrm{in}})+\mathsf Z_1(r_{\mathrm{in}})+k^{-1}\mathsf Z_2(r_{\mathrm{in}})+\cdots.
\]
Comparison in \eqref{audrev-eq-layered-riccati} yields
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-recursion}
 \mathcal S(\mathsf Z_1)=\mathsf Z_0',\qquad
 \mathcal S(\mathsf Z_j)=\mathsf Z_{j-1}'-
 \sum_{\substack{r+t=j\\r,t\geq1}}\mathsf Z_rA^{-1}\mathsf Z_t
 \quad(j\geq2).
\end{equation}
Applying \(\mathcal S^{-1}\) to \(\mathsf Z_0'\) in
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-recursion} gives the complete first coefficient
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-first}
 \begin{split}
 b&=\frac{\mu^2\lambda'+(\lambda^2-2\mu^2)\mu'}{\ell_*^2},\\
 \mathsf Z_1&=\begin{pmatrix}
 \mu'(1-a)+b&0&ib\\
 0&\mu'/2&0\\
 -ib&0&\mu'(1+a)+b
 \end{pmatrix}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
An explicit complete matrix formula at the second order is
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-second}
 \begin{split}
 Q&=\mathsf Z_1'-\mathsf Z_1A^{-1}\mathsf Z_1,\\
 \mathsf Z_2&=\tfrac12Q-\tfrac14(N^*Q+QN)+\tfrac14N^*QN,\\
 (\mathsf Z_2)_{22}&=\tfrac14\mu''-\frac{(\mu')^2}{8\mu}.
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Since \(\mathsf Z_1\) is given by \eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-first},
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-second} contains every term quadratic in first derivatives and
every second-derivative term;
in particular the nonlinear term \(-\mathsf Z_1A^{-1}\mathsf Z_1\) is retained.
After subtraction of terms involving only the values and first
derivatives, the second-order off-diagonal observation is
\[
 (\mathsf Z_2^{\mathrm{top}})_{13}/i
 =\frac{\mu^2\lambda''+(3\lambda^2+4\lambda\mu)\mu''}
        {2\ell_*^2}.
\]

In the recursion at order \(j\), every factor in the sum has
index at most \(j-1\). Differentiating the coefficients of
\(\mathcal S^{-1}\) also produces only normal derivatives of lower order.
Induction therefore shows that the part containing the new
\(j\)-th normal derivatives is exactly
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-highest}
 \mathsf Z_j^{\mathrm{top}}
 =\mathcal S^{-j}\left((\partial_\lambda\mathsf Z_0)\lambda^{(j)}
                         +(\partial_\mu\mathsf Z_0)\mu^{(j)}\right),
 \qquad \lambda^{(j)}=\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\lambda,
 \quad \mu^{(j)}=\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\mu.
\end{equation}
The inversion needed here is for the two isotropic parameters,
not for freely prescribed derivatives of an arbitrary impedance
matrix. To see its rank explicitly, in the \((1,3)\) plane let
\[
 U_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&i\\-i&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
In that plane \(\mathsf Z_0=2\mu I_2-\mu(1-a)U_0\),
\(N^*U_0=U_0N=0\), and
\(\partial_\lambda\mathsf Z_0=2\mu^2\ell_*^{-2}U_0\). Hence
\[
 \mathcal S^{-j}(\partial_\lambda\mathsf Z_0)=
 2^{1-j}\mu^2\ell_*^{-2}U_0,
 \qquad (\mathsf Z_j^{\mathrm{top}})_{22}=2^{-j}\mu^{(j)}.
\]
More explicitly, the two scalar observations satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-two-observations}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 (\mathsf Z_j^{\mathrm{top}})_{22}\\
 (\mathsf Z_j^{\mathrm{top}})_{13}/i
 \end{pmatrix}
 =\begin{pmatrix}
 2^{-j}&0\\
 \beta_j&2^{1-j}\mu^2/\ell_*^2
 \end{pmatrix}
 \begin{pmatrix}\mu^{(j)}\\\lambda^{(j)}\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 \beta_j=2^{-j}\left(j(j+1)a^2-
                \frac{2\mu(2\lambda+3\mu)}{\ell_*^2}\right).
\end{equation}
Its determinant is
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-jet-determinant}
 2^{1-2j}\frac{\mu^2}{\ell_*^2}>0.
\end{equation}
One recovers \(\mu^{(j)}\) first and then \(\lambda^{(j)}\).
There is no division by \(\lambda\); negative \(\lambda\) and
\(\lambda=0\) allowed by \eqref{eq:convexity} are included.
At normal order zero, recovery begins with the principal
DN symbol through

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-order-zero}
 \mu=(\mathsf Z_0)_{22},\qquad d=(\mathsf Z_0)_{11},\qquad
 \lambda=\frac{3d\mu-4\mu^2}{2\mu-d},
 \qquad 2\mu-d=\frac{2\mu^2}{\ell_*}>0.
\end{equation}


The highest coefficients also have an energy check with the same
outward sign. For the frozen stable solution the divergence amplitude,
after removal of \(e^{iky_1}\), is
\(k(1-a)e^{-k r_{\mathrm{in}}}(if_1-f_3)\). An infinitesimal perturbation
\(\delta\lambda(r_{\mathrm{in}})=r_{\mathrm{in}}^j\delta\lambda^{(j)}/j!\) in the DN energy
therefore has coefficient
\((1-a)^2/2^{j+1}=2^{1-j}\mu^2/\ell_*^2\) at degree \(k^{1-j}\).
For the transverse solution the corresponding coefficient of
\(\delta\mu^{(j)}\) is \(2^{-j}\). The integral used is
\(\int_0^\infty r_{\mathrm{in}}^je^{-2k r_{\mathrm{in}}}\,d r_{\mathrm{in}}=j!/(2k)^{j+1}\).
This checks the highest linear coefficients; the full nonlinear
recursion is \eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-recursion}.


For an independent check of the nontrivial off-diagonal coefficient
at \emph{every} order, write $t=k r_{\mathrm{in}}$ and use the frozen stable
field $u_f=e^{iky_1-t}(I_3-tN)f$.  After division by
$ke^{iky_1-t}$, its strain amplitude is denoted by $E_f(t)$.
Direct differentiation gives
\begin{equation}\label{auditv-eq-energy-polynomial}
 \frac{2\overline{E_{e_1}(t)}:E_{e_3}(t)}{i}
  =8a^2t^2-8a^2t+3a^2-2a-1.
\end{equation}
For the first variation of the physical elastic energy with respect
to the $j$-th normal derivatives of the Lam\'e parameters, the integral
of this polynomial is
\begin{equation}\label{auditv-eq-energy-beta}
 \frac{1}{j!}\int_0^\infty t^je^{-2t}
  (8a^2t^2-8a^2t+3a^2-2a-1)\,dt
 =2^{-j}\left(j(j+1)a^2-
       \frac{2\mu(2\lambda+3\mu)}{\ell_*^2}\right)=\beta_j.
\end{equation}
The transverse shear amplitude gives the independent coefficient
$2^{-j}$ of $\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\mu$, while the divergence amplitude
$(1-a)(if_1-f_3)$ gives the coefficient
$2^{1-j}\mu^2/\ell_*^2$ of $\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\lambda$.
Thus the rank-two recovery matrix is checked by two genuinely
different mechanisms: Sylvester recursion and physical energy
variation. This independent calculation verifies the \emph{coefficients
of the highest-order unknown normal derivatives}; it does not replace
the full
nonlinear symbol recursion.
<!-- end proof-42 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:appendix-true-dn-symbol}
For a smooth strongly convex isotropic elasticity operator on a
smooth three-dimensional domain, its outward conormal
Dirichlet-to-Neumann map is a classical tangential pseudodifferential
operator of order one. In an orthonormal frame at a boundary point,
its principal symbol equals \(k\mathsf Z_0\) from
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-principal}, \(k=|\xi|\).
For every \(j\geq1\), after terms determined by the normal
derivatives of the Lam\'e parameters of orders at most \(j-1\),
their tangential derivatives and
the known boundary geometry have been subtracted, the new contribution
to the homogeneous symbol of degree \(1-j\) is
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-new-jet-symbol}.
The recursively constructed symbol equals the full symbol of the
actual outward traction DN map modulo a smoothing symbol.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-43: body_md -->
The normal-flux computation and the boundary Poisson parametrix
provide the required identification. We retain the full physical
flux in an inward distance chart as follows.
Put \(n=\nabla r_{\mathrm{in}}\), \(w^\alpha=\nabla y^\alpha\),
and let \(J\) be the positive coordinate volume density. For the
isotropic tensor
\[
 C_{ijk\ell}=\lambda\delta_{ij}\delta_{k\ell}
       +\mu(\delta_{ik}\delta_{j\ell}+\delta_{i\ell}\delta_{jk}),
\]
define
\[
 A_{ik}=C_{ijk\ell}n_jn_\ell,\qquad
 B^\alpha_{ik}=C_{ijk\ell}n_jw^\alpha_\ell,\qquad
 D^{\alpha\beta}_{ik}=C_{ijk\ell}w^\alpha_jw^\beta_\ell.
\]
The minor and major symmetries of \(C\) imply that the tangential
flux is \((B^\alpha)^T\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}u+D^{\alpha\beta}\partial_\beta u\).
Writing \(v=A\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}u+B^\alpha\partial_\alpha u\), the divergence
equation in these coordinates is the exact system
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-curved-flux}
 \begin{split}
 \partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}u={}&A^{-1}v-A^{-1}B^\alpha\partial_\alpha u,\\
 \partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}v={}&-\frac{\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}J}{J}v
 -J^{-1}\partial_\alpha\big(J(B^\alpha)^TA^{-1}v\big)\\
 &+J^{-1}\partial_\alpha\big(
 J((B^\alpha)^TA^{-1}B^\beta-D^{\alpha\beta})
                 \partial_\beta u\big).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
Repeated tangential indices are summed. Its coefficients involve
the material values and their tangential derivatives, but no
explicit unknown normal derivatives of the Lam\'e parameters. The term \(\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}J/J\)
is known geometry.

For the stable graph \(v=-\mathcal P(r_{\mathrm{in}})u\), differentiating and
substituting \eqref{audrev-eq-curved-flux} gives the operator Riccati
identity, modulo smoothing terms in a microlocal construction,
\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-curved-riccati}
 \begin{split}
 \partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}\mathcal P={}&\mathcal PA^{-1}
       (\mathcal P+B^\beta\partial_\beta)
       -\frac{\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}J}{J}\mathcal P\\
 &-J^{-1}\partial_\alpha
       \big(J(B^\alpha)^TA^{-1}\mathcal P\big)\\
 &-J^{-1}\partial_\alpha\big(
 J((B^\alpha)^TA^{-1}B^\beta-D^{\alpha\beta})
                                  \partial_\beta\big).
 \end{split}
\end{equation}
All products in the operator Riccati identity \eqref{audrev-eq-curved-riccati} are operator compositions. For
left quantization, their symbols are expanded by
\[
 p\#q\sim\sum_\gamma
 \frac{1}{i^{|\gamma|}\gamma!}
 (\partial_\xi^\gamma p)(\partial_y^\gamma q).
\]
At a point, in the orthonormal frame fixed above, let
\(k=|\xi|_{g_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}}\). The leading homogeneous symbol of
\(\mathcal P\) is \(k\mathsf Z_0\) from
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-principal}. Its leading linearization on
the coefficient of degree \(1-j\) is \(k\mathcal S\).

The orders of normal derivatives entering the complete symbol
recursion follow from \eqref{audrev-eq-curved-riccati}. Let
\(p_{1-r}\) be the homogeneous term of degree \(1-r\); by
induction it contains normal derivatives of the material coefficients of order at most \(r\),
together with known geometry and tangential derivatives of those
traces. At degree \(2-j\), the left-hand side of
\eqref{audrev-eq-curved-riccati} differentiates
\(p_{2-j}\) normally, so it can introduce the new order-\(j\)
material derivative. A composition term with no derivatives of its symbols containing
two lower symbol coefficients has indices adding to \(j\),
each at most \(j-1\). A nonprincipal composition term of length
\(|\gamma|\geq1\) has coefficient indices adding to
\(j-|\gamma|\), and therefore contains normal derivatives of the material coefficients of
order at most \(j-1\). Tangential derivatives do not increase
normal derivatives of the material parameters of order at most $j-1$. Terms with \(\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}J/J\), derivatives of \(k\),
or derivatives of the known moving frame likewise use only
previous normal traces. Finally, differentiation of the
coefficients of a prior \(\mathcal S^{-1}\) introduces first
normal derivatives multiplying derivatives of lower order, and cannot create a new
pure order-\(j\) normal derivative.

It follows inductively that, after subtracting all terms known
from the normal traces of orders \(0,\ldots,j-1\) on the patch
and their tangential derivatives, the new term is precisely

\begin{equation}\label{audrev-eq-boundary-new-jet-symbol}
 k^{1-j}\mathcal S^{-j}
      ((\partial_\lambda\mathsf Z_0)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\lambda
        +(\partial_\mu\mathsf Z_0)\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^j\mu).
\end{equation}

Thus \eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-jet-determinant} applies also to the
curved, tangentially variable problem. Subtracting the known lower
normal traces and their tangential derivatives is necessary:
self-adjointness of the DN operator does not make every term of a
left-quantized symbol Hermitian. For example, the anti-plane scalar
equation with coefficient \(\mu(y,r_{\mathrm{in}})\), at positive tangential
frequency, has the first two lower symbols
\[
 \frac{\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}\mu-i\mu_y}{2},\qquad
 \frac{\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}^2\mu+\mu_{yy}}4-
       \frac{(\partial_{r_{\mathrm{in}}}\mu)^2+(\partial_y\mu)^2}{8\mu}.
\]
The known tangential terms are retained and subtracted in the
recursion, rather than suppressed by a symmetry claim.


Identification with the physical DN symbol relies on the classical
smooth Dirichlet--Poisson parametrix principle
for strongly elliptic second-order matrix systems: if the decaying
frozen normal solution space has the same dimension as the boundary
data space and its boundary-value projection is invertible for all
nonzero tangential covectors, the true Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator
has the full classical symbol of the stable conormal graph modulo a
smoothing operator.  Repeated stable characteristic roots are
permitted, provided the stable and unstable spectral subspaces are
separated and vary smoothly.  Here the principal elastic
characteristic determinant is
$\mu^2(\lambda+2\mu)(\rho_{\mathrm{char}}^2+|\xi|^2)^3$;
the stable and unstable roots are $\rho_{\mathrm{char}}=\pm i|\xi|$.
The explicit stable solution
$e^{-|\xi|r_{\mathrm{in}}}(I_3-|\xi|r_{\mathrm{in}}N)f$ has boundary value exactly $f$;
thus the Dirichlet complementing condition is satisfied despite
$N^2=0$ and $N\ne0$.  Strong convexity, Korn's inequality and
smooth Dirichlet regularity supply the required global correction
operator.  In particular, one must identify the formal Riccati
symbol with the \emph{physical} conormal DN map by the Poisson
construction, not by symbol algebra alone.


The stable Poisson parametrix is constructed from the recursively
determined symbol, and its smoothing error is corrected through
the Dirichlet problem. The frozen inward first-order evolution has three stable and three
unstable modes, with normalized eigenvalues \(-1\) and \(+1\),
respectively.
The stable Dirichlet projection is invertible by
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-jordan}; the stable and unstable spaces
are separated even when either has a Jordan block. The usual
classical boundary construction therefore solves
\eqref{audrev-eq-curved-riccati} successively by the invertible
\(\mathcal S\), sums its symbol by Borel summation, and constructs
a Poisson parametrix whose boundary trace is the identity modulo
smoothing. Its frozen leading term is
\(e^{-k r_{\mathrm{in}}}(I_3-k r_{\mathrm{in}}N)\). The exponential decay absorbs the polynomial
Jordan factor.

Cutting this parametrix off inside a collar produces, away from
the boundary, a source smoothing in the boundary data; the
near-boundary error is smoothing by the successive construction.
The smooth Dirichlet inverse corrects the collar-cutoff source and the
near-boundary smoothing error. Its existence follows from coercivity of
the physical Dirichlet problem: decompose
the elastic energy into trace-free and trace parts:
\[
 \lambda(\operatorname{tr} E)^2+2\mu|E|^2
 =2\mu\left|E-\tfrac13(\operatorname{tr} E)I_3\right|^2
     +(\lambda+2\mu/3)(\operatorname{tr} E)^2.
\]
The uniform bounds in \eqref{eq:convexity}, zero-trace Korn's
inequality and Poincar\'e's inequality give coercivity; the usual
Dirichlet existence and elliptic regularity give the correcting
inverse. Consequently the true outward DN map and the constructed
\(\mathcal P(0)\) have the same complete classical symbol modulo
smoothing. No normal derivative of the full impedance is assumed
to be observed: successive lower homogeneous terms of the given
DN map are the observations.
<!-- end proof-43 -->
<!-- proof-44: body_md -->
The principal symbol in
Proposition~\ref{prop:appendix-true-dn-symbol} first determines the
boundary values: in the orthonormal frame,
\(\mu=(\mathsf Z_0)_{22}\) and \(d=(\mathsf Z_0)_{11}\), so
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-order-zero} recovers \(\lambda\).
Localizing equal full DN maps on a boundary patch gives equal
complete symbols there. The principal recovery and then
\eqref{audrev-eq-boundary-jet-determinant} prove equality of all
normal traces by induction. Their tangential derivatives agree
because the recovered normal derivatives of $\lambda_j$ and
$\mu_j$ agree as smooth functions on the boundary patch.
The argument is local on every boundary component; interactions
between disjoint patches or distinct components contribute only
smooth kernels and do not alter the local symbol. On a hole
boundary the distance is still taken into \(\Omega\), so the
outward traction remains \(-v\). This proves the equality of all boundary
derivatives without a connected-boundary assumption. The
coefficient difference is therefore flat on \(\partial\Omega\),
as required by the extension construction in
Lemma~\ref{lem:common-extension}.
<!-- end proof-44 -->
